## Funding proposal package for FP247

### Summary

b) No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or fiscal point(s);

c) Secretary's assessment;

d) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;

e) Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;

f) Gender documentation.

### Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.

• Accredited Entities are required to submit a completed funding proposal based on the data provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the project proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in the updated funding proposal.

• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 150 pages. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time frame.

• The recommended font is Arial, size 11.

• Under the GCF’s Funding Proposal Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to any confidentiality requirements. Accredited Entities are required to inform the IDP Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G-4.

Please submit the completed proposal to:

fundingproposal@unfccc.org

Please use the following name convention for the file name: "FP [Accredited Entity/Short Name]/[Country/Region] (YYYY/MM/DD)"

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 3

Burkina Faso (Upper Volta), Mali, and Niger have high vulnerability to climate change and limited addresses to these challenges, according to the Notre Dame University Global Adaptation Initiative (ND-GAIN) ranking. These countries are particularly vulnerable due to their dependence on natural resources impacting food security and infrastructure. The IPC/UNICEF 2018 Humanitarian Report highlights that West Africa is one of the regions most significantly affected by climate change in the last 50 years, with more frequent droughts and fewer cold ones. Projections for the end of the 21st century predict temperature increases of 3°C to 6°C with the highest increase being observed in Mali (4.8°C) followed by Niger (4.7°C). The frequency of extreme heatwaves is expected to increase, followed by some recovery. Future projections suggest more extreme rainfall events.

In response, the programme aims to promote climate-resilient and low-carbon economies in these countries by encouraging adaptation to climate change knowledge, and investment capacities. The initiative seeks to align with the Paris Agreement, national targets, and specific Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The enhanced institutional framework will be based on a multi-stakeholder approach involving governments, civil society organizations, and private sector actors.

The programme builds upon the UNDP-designed Local Climate Investment Fund (LCIF) in Burkina Faso, Mali, and Niger. LCIF is a standard mechanism that channels climate finance to local governments and communities through the Public-Private Climate Investment Facility (PPCIF). The PPCIF consists of financial tools to cover additional costs of making investments climate-resilient and low-carbon. It also includes a risk-sharing component for public sector actors, including local governments.

To deliver its objectives, the programme builds on the institutional experience of the UNDP-designed Local Climate Investment Fund (LCIF) in Burkina Faso, Mali, and Niger, and SDAID’s expertise in climate-focused blended financing instruments in West Africa. LCIF is a standard mechanism that channels climate finance to local governments and communities through the Public-Private Climate Investment Facility (PPCIF). The PPCIF consists of financial tools to cover additional costs of making investments climate-resilient and low-carbon. It also includes a risk-sharing component for public sector actors, including local governments.

The programme will reach the population of up to 85 million people through a combination of:

1. Dedicated technical assistance and capacity building (Component 1), which will support the enabling conditions for enhancing the capacities of local governments, local actors and communities to identify, plan, finance and implement climate-resilient investments.
2. Performance-based climate resilience grants (Component 2.1), that will cover the additional costs of public sector actors in implementing climate-resilient investments, including capacity building and planning processes of target local governments.
3. Blended financing instruments (Component 2.2) which will address access to adaptation finance constraints of a wider range of local private sector actors, such as local SMEs and local producers’ organizations and generate a pipeline of private sector-sponsored investments aligned with adaptation priorities of larger communities.


---
**Page 8**
---

The programme will receive approx. EUR 28 million (through GCF proceeds and expected co-financing) as subnational adaptation finance for adaptation investments in the four target countries. Furthermore, the integration of the financing mechanism within the existing national climate change institutions/structures (Blended Finance Facilities) will allow to continue the deployment in other local governments at the end of the 5 years of the programme. Working in 4 countries within the sub-region will provide economies of scale and will contribute to the development of a regional GCF-ECMIF portfolio for adaptation financing as well as to contribute to the formalization of financing models to channel and deploy climate finance at the local level.

# PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1 Country Context (1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### Regional outlook for West Africa

West Africa is one of the world's most vulnerable regions to climate variability and change<sup>1</sup>. Increasing temperatures and more frequent extreme weather events are already affecting livelihoods, health, and economic and governance systems. Extreme climate variability has been observed in West Africa since the 1970s that have had significant impacts on food security. The region is expected to experience a greater increase than the global average, a trend expected to continue with the gradual warming in the Sahel region. Along the coast of West Africa, extreme weather events are projected to become more frequent and intense. This will impact the coastal population, urban centres and ports, coastal aquifers, and the agriculture and fisheries sectors (Harley, 2016). The West African climate is also expected to be more variable in terms of rainfall patterns and intensity. These regions have a high level of vulnerability that particularly affects the most fragile communities. These trends that are expected to continue will increase pressure on natural resources and exacerbate existing vulnerabilities.

### Mali

Mali is among the world's poorest nations, with over 40% of its population living in poverty. It has approximately 19.6 million people (2015) and relies heavily on agriculture, which accounts for 30% GDP and employs a significant portion of the workforce. However, only 14% of Mali's land is suitable for agriculture, making sustainable land management critical.

Mali's geography is divided between the wet Sahara and Sahel in the north and the wetter, more economically active southern regions. The country experiences two distinct seasons: a long dry season and a shorter wet season. Rainfall varies significantly across the country, with the highest rainfall typically in the south but less than 100 mm in climate patterns are influenced by global phenomena like El Niño and La Niña.

### Burkina Faso

Climate change projections indicate a general increase in mean daily temperatures by +2°C to +3.0°C, affecting agricultural productivity and water availability. The country's economy is heavily dependent on agriculture for food security, transport, is vulnerable to extreme weather, and hydroelectricity production could decrease by up to 27% by 2025. Low-lying areas are particularly at risk from flooding due to increased rainfall intensity. These changes will likely lead to declining agricultural productivity and reduced water availability.

Burkina Faso faces significant challenges due to its limited natural resources and highly variable climate. With 48% of the population living below the poverty line, the country struggles with food security and economic opportunities. Burkina Faso is diversifying its economy, focusing on biomass, hydroelectricity, and solar energy; yet only 17% of the population has access to electricity. The country's agricultural sector remains highly vulnerable due to climate variability linked to subsistence farming, though much of the land is degraded. Climate-related issues such as drought, dust storms, floods, and landslides are common, leading to soil erosion, reduced crop yields, increased water scarcity, and disease outbreaks. While local efforts to improve water retention and cultivation resilience exist, they remain small-scale and insufficient for addressing the country's overall agricultural productivity, including technical capacity, financial support, water storage, crop diversification, and soil restoration.

Burkina Faso has a dry tropical climate with significant seasonal and annual variations influenced by the Inter-Tropical Convergence Zone. The rainy season varies from two months in the north to six months in the south. The dry season

### NIGER

The Republic of Niger spans 1,277,596 km² with three-quarters of its terrain being desert. In 2019, Niger's population was approximately 23 million, with only 10% women and 70% under the age of 25. The population is predominantly rural (83.7%), concentrated mainly in the southern region. The economy is undiversified and heavily dependent on agriculture, which accounts for about 40% of GDP.

Niger's climate is semi-arid with a dry season from October to May when temperatures range from 21°C to 33°C and a rainy season from June to September. Rainfall varies significantly between regions; it averages around 500 mm in the north but can be as high as 900 mm in the south, particularly in August. Climate change projections indicate a mean annual temperature rise of 1-1.5°C by 2040–2069, with more frequent and intense heatwaves.

Climate change impacts Niger significantly. The agriculture sector, heavily reliant on rain-fed practices, faces water and heat stress, pest outbreaks, and ecosystem deterioration, leading to reduced yields and food insecurity. Water supply is already a major challenge in many regions, particularly in the north where rainfall is scarce. This situation exacerbates conflicts and conflicts.

The energy sector, dependent on hydropower, is vulnerable to climate variability affecting supply and demand for electricity. The health sector faces increased risks from heatwaves, droughts, floods, and droughts impacting water, food, and air quality, and rising temperatures increasing aggression and violence.

### CÔTE D'IVOIRE

Unlike the three other countries mentioned above, Côte d'Ivoire is located along the Gulf of Guinea and features diverse landscapes. The rainy season in the south and savannah in the north; high rainfall in the south supports agriculture, including cocoa, cashews, and coffee, key to the economy. Around half of the workforce is employed in agriculture, critical despite its low contribution to GDP.

The country's economic diversification has been slow, with nearly half of the population living in urban areas while many others rely on cash and subsistence farming.

The economy is diversifying, with growth in telecommunications, information technology, and trade. However, social-economic disparity remains high, with 29% of the population living below the poverty line and 38.1% without electricity. Heavy reliance on agriculture and environmental degradation are significant challenges.

Côte d'Ivoire's climate transitions from a humid equatorial climate in the south to a dry tropical climate in the north. The rainy season lasts from June to October, with annual temperatures ranging from 24°C to 28°C. The energy sector, heavily dependent on hydropower, is vulnerable to climate variability affecting supply and demand for electricity.

Climate change impacts include disrupted rainfall cycles, reduced crop stability, increased frequency of extreme weather events such as droughts and floods, increasing water stress and poverty. Coastal areas face salinization, flooding, and erosion, causing significant environmental degradation and human displacement, respiratory infections, and diarrhea diseases.

Locally led adaptation action for climate resilient livelihoods

The programme addresses climate risks in Mali, Niger, Burkina Faso, and Ivory Coast by empowering local governments to make adaptation decisions based on local priorities. It supports locally-led investments in seven key areas: water resources management, food security, energy access, transport infrastructure, urban resilience, land use efficiencies and renewables, climate-resilient infrastructure, and coastal areas (Ivory Coast). Each country has developed tailored adaptation investments through consultations, considering specific socio-economic and

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 7

B (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

Rural communities in the target countries are particularly vulnerable to climate change because of their high dependence on natural resources for livelihoods and food security. These communities have limited capacity to adapt to shocks and stresses. Faced with these challenges, local actors and local governments can understand and respond to the needs of rural communities by identifying climate risks and opportunities at a local level. When local actors are able to identify climate risks in terms of local specificsities and develop solutions, mobilizing stakeholders for locally appropriate adaptation actions becomes possible. This is particularly true when local actors become agents of change and participate in e + consultation with the communities – the climate resilience of rural communities will be increased.

If response capacities to awareness of climate change impacts are increased at the local level and country-based national strategies are developed, then the climate resilience of rural communities and economies will be increased. This is because the enhanced institutional, technical and financial capacity of local governments and local actors will contribute towards the development of resilient and diversified livelihoods options (ARA 1.0), health and wellbeing, water and food security (ARA 2.0), infrastructure and built environment (ARA 3.0), ecosystems and eco-system services (ARA 4.0), while paving the way for the implementation of national strategies at a local level.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.01 PAGE B

## Private sector engagement in climate action commitments, key local stakeholders such as small MSMEs or local producers/organisations have access to adaptation finance and assistance to contribute to the resilience building efforts of their communities and economies.

### The programme will lead to the implementation of both public and private adaptation investments of different type through a) dedicated technical assistance and capacity building that will support the enabling conditions for enhancing the capacities of local governments, local actors and communities to identify, finance and implement catalytic projects; b) Performance-based climate resilience grants that will cover the additional costs of public locally led climate-resilient investments in the four countries (Component 1); c) Business readiness grants to enhance the capacity of target of local governments (Component 2).

### The programme aims at increasing access to adaptation finance constraints of a wider range of local private sector actors, such as small MSMEs and local producers' organisations and generate a pipeline of private sector investments in climate action.

The performance-based climate resilience grant system (PBCRSG) provides a country-based mechanism to channel funds to cover the additional costs of making public investments climate resilient or of new investment for adaptation purposes. The PBCRSG is managed at local governments, which will leverage on common structures and deconcentrated state services to ensure that the grants are effectively delivered in the communities they serve.

Facilities in each country will strengthen existing institutions/structures dealing with private sector financing to design and deploy dedicated adaptation windows to the benefit of a wider range of local private sector actors, thus addressing access to adaptation finance barriers for the private sector to actively participate in local climate action.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

The integration of the financing mechanisms within inter-governmental transfer systems (PICRO Facilities) and existing private sector financing institutions/structures (Blended Finance Facilities) will allow to continue the deployment in the GCF results areas of projects that are aligned with the GCF strategic objectives. This type of blended finance instrument will provide economies of scale, help generate and test tools with greater replication potential, particularly in UEMOA countries, as a model for financing as well as to contribute to the formalization of financing models to channel and deploy climate finance at the country level.

### B.3 Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

This project will promote climate-resilient communities and local economies by empowering local governments and stakeholders with increased access to adaptation finance, awareness, and climate risk management skills. This will enable them to better align their development plans with national priorities, including the Paris Agreement objectives, national targets, and SDG targets. Enhanced institutional, technical, and financial capacities of local governments and communities will be developed through capacity building (AIA 1.0), health and well-being, water and food security (AIA 2.0), infrastructure (AIA 3.0), and ecosystem services (AIA 4.0), integrating climate resilience into standard development practices.

#### Component 1: The awareness and institutional capacities of local governments, local actors and communities are strengthened and promote local adaptation responses

**Output 1:** Awareness of climate change risks and impacts is enhanced at the local level

- **Activity 1.1:** Assessments will determine local stakeholders' needs for awareness-raising and capacity building on climate change risks and impacts.
- **Target audiences include local governments, state services, SMEs, cooperatives, community organizations, and civil society groups in Burkina Faso, Niger, Mali, and Senegal.**
- **Responsibilities:** UNCDF (TA) and National Agencies (FEI, ANFICT, ANIFCT, ANIDDC, as EEIs).

- **Activity 1.2:** Based on identified needs, sensitization activities such as events and workshops will be organized for local stakeholders and communities.
- **Campaigns will raise awareness about climate change risks and adaptation strategies in the context of national development plans.**
- **Leaders from different ethnic groups will help cultural sensitivity and local buy-in for effective climate adaptation planning.**
- **Responsibilities:** UNCDF (TA) and National Agencies (FEI, ANFICT, ANIFCT, ANIDDC, as EEIs).

- **Activity 1.3:** A local outreach and communication strategy will be developed and implemented.
- **Communication materials will be disseminated through channels like local radio and newspapers, led by local governments and private sector partners in Burkina Faso, Niger, Mali, and Senegal.**
- **Responsibilities:** UNCDF (TA) and National Agencies (FEI, ANFICT, ANIFCT, ANIDDC, as EEIs).

**Output 2: Climate data availability and access are improved for risk informed local decision making**

- **Activity 2.1:** Localized analyses of climate risks, including hazards, exposure, and vulnerabilities will be conducted.
- **This includes climate downscaling and risk analysis for past and future projections to identify climate stressors and**

**Output 3:** Capacities of local governments to plan, budget, implement and monitor for effective locally-led adaptation are enhanced

Responsibilities: UNCDF (TA) and National Agencies (FE, ANFICT, ANICT, DGGD), as EEs

* **Activity 3.2:** Develop and integrate Annual Investment Plans (AIPs) for adaptation into local development plans. * **Activity 3.5:** Develop a Service Delivery Model for Local Governments to access Blended Climate Resilience Grants (BCRGs) features. Activities include developing communication materials, awareness raising workshops, and training sessions with local government.

This aims to build a sustainable value chain by providing technical assistance on climate-resilient agricultural practices, climate-smart processing SMEs, and SMEs in climate-sensitive or green economy sectors. Training topics include climate-smart agriculture, sustainable value addition, and cash flow management implemented with state services and local government departments.

## Activity 4.3: Technical assistance (TA) for eligible local actors to develop compliant, bankable investment proposals.

**Responsibilities:** BOAD (AE) and CCT UEMOA (EE)

### Sub-component 2: Country-based financing mechanisms to access and channel climate finance at the local level are established and enable locally led resilience building investments in priority sectors.

The performance-based-financing mechanism displayed in the framework of the programme to finance locally-led adaptation projects is based on the publication "Financing Local Adaptation to Climate Change: Experience with Performance-Based Climate Resilience Grants" as cited as supplementary material to the National Adaptation Plan Technical Guidelines. In 2022, UNDP and UEMOA developed a performance-based climate resilience grants (Requirements and guidelines) was developed using the methodology and experiences of the UNCDF and UEMOA.

The financing mechanism operates through PBRCGRs, which provide financial top-ups to cover costs for climate-resilient investments and adaptation. These grants target local governments, utilizing communal structures and state services. Local governments are eligible if they have a population of at least 50% of the total population in the region (20%), the population of the project area is less than or equal to 1 million people, and surface area of the project area is less than or equal to 50 thousand square kilometers.

Target local governments will be selected during programme inception in consultation with the LGUA. Technical assistance will be provided by UEMOA to support the development of PBRCGRs. The mechanism will have three phases: integrate learning into implementation, Aligned with each country's inter-governmental transfer systems and decentralization, the mechanism is scalable and can be progressively mainstreamed.

PBRCGR allocation formula per country

| Country | Initial allocation criteria | Performance measures |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Burkina Faso | 50% granted following SEI assessment of poverty, vulnerability, resilience, holding of local government accounts, rate of collection of local and central government taxes, and proportion (20%) of the population in the region (20%), the population of the project area is less than or equal to 1 million people, and surface area of the project area is less than or equal to 50 thousand square kilometers | 40% based on performance |
| Niger | 30% for performance weighted as a proportion of the total population in the region (20%), the population of the project area is less than or equal to 1 million people, and surface area of the project area is less than or equal to 50 thousand square kilometers | 40% based on performance |
| Mali | 30% granted following SEI assessment of poverty, vulnerability, resilience, holding of local government accounts, rate of collection of local and central government taxes, and proportion (20%) of the population in the region (20%), the population of the project area is less than or equal to 1 million people, and surface area of the project area is less than or equal to 50 thousand square kilometers | 30% for performance weighted as a proportion of the total population in the region (20%), the population of the project area is less than or equal to 1 million people, and surface area of the project area is less than or equal to 50 thousand square kilometers |
| Ivory Coast | PBRCGR allocation of USD 3.75 million per year | 30% taken into account from 2nd year of implementation |

### Sub-Component 2.2: Blended Finance Facilities are established in the target countries to catalyse adaptation finance for revenue generating investments at the local level

This sub-component aims to provide sustainable and innovative financing for local private sector-led projects that address climate priorities identified by local governments. Blended Finance Facilities, complementing the PBCRG system, will leverage SGB funds and local financial sector products to support SMEs and producer organizations in climate resilience efforts.

These facilities offer technical assistance and repayable/repayable grants, ensuring funds are accessible through cooperation with local MFIs and maintaining traceability and coherence. ## Output 2: Blended Finance Facilities are established and revenue generating adaptation investments are financed in line with the menu of eligible investments in each target country.

### Activity 7.1: Established Blended Finance Facilities, including governance bodies like project selection and investment committees, ensuring subnational and private sector actor inclusion. Technical assistance will support setup, aligning with AE procedures and GCP standards, defining operational modalities, investment criteria, environmental and social safeguards, and establishing a national planning process.

**Responsibilities:** BOAD (A) and CCT UEMOA (EE)

### Activity 7.2: Award and implement private sector-led adaptation projects in target areas through reimbursable from public funds or blended finance mechanisms. This will include developing guidelines for applications to regular submissions. Facilities may also finance revenue-generating public projects if national laws allow local government borrowing. Eligibility criteria, including the use of a risk-based approach (e.g., Climate Risk Index), will be defined in the GCP (C). A dual key approach will ensure evidence-based decisions accounting for climate risks and opportunities.

**Responsibilities:** BOAD (A) and CCT UEMOA (EE)

Based on the scoping and design studies of the LoCAL mechanism conducted in each country (see Annexes 2), this approach aims to enhance local government capacity for adaptation financing. It enhances local governments' and stakeholders' capacities through awareness creation, mainstreaming support, finance channeling, and implementation. GCP will define a menu of eligible investments, including climate risk assessments, project identification, annual adaptation monitoring, annual performance assessment, and institutionalization of PBCRG Facilitators, ensuring that all public funds are used for climate-resilient projects. This menu also tests innovative lending and lending instruments, setting up Blended Finance Facilities within existing institutions.

The following tools will be provided to local governments (PBCRG Facilitators) and project selection and investment committees:

- **Climate Data and Risk Indexes:** Local governments will receive downloaded projections of climate variables to build indices of future climate hazards, exposures, and vulnerabilities. These tailored indexes will inform local climate adaptation strategies.
- **Mainstreaming Support:** This menu defines appropriate areas for increased resilience without specifying individual projects. Local governments and private sector actors will be supported in mainstreaming climate considerations into their planning processes and investment decisions.
- **C4CC/MAE Framework:** This tool will be provided to target local governments to track GCF-financed investments and adaptation benefits at the community level. It helps evaluate investment decisions and integrates learning into future projects.

### Annual Performance Assessments (APAs): APA analysis tracks local government compliance with minimum conditions for accessing funds, including climate risk assessments, project identification, and annual monitoring. This will impact the PRCSM amount for the following year and identify capacity-building needs.
- **Budgeting:** The budgeting process will be streamlined to ensure that all public funds are used for climate-resilient projects. Blended Finance Facilities will be assessed for financial sustainability and climate resilience impact. This ensures evidence-based decisions that account for climate risks and opportunities, detailing each project's climate rationale and expected benefits.

## B

The West African Development Bank (BOAD) will be the Accredited Entity (AE) responsible for the implementation of the programme in close collaboration with the Executing Entities (EE), CCF, UEMOA and national institutions as highlighted below. The BOAD will also act as the Implementing Partner, providing key support and expertise and ensuring the effective implementation of the LoCAL standard – in consultation with the AE and EE.

### Legal and agreements between the GCF, AE and EE

BOAD and GCF: As the Accredited Entity of the programme, BOAD will sign a Funded Activity Agreement (Trust Agreement) with GCF.

BOAD and EE(s): BOAD will sign a Subsidiary Agreement with the EE(s) to grant GCF resources for the implementation of specific activities of the programme in accordance with the Funded Activity Agreement.

### Institutional arrangements and governance structure of the PBCRG Facilities (Component 2.5)

#### Executing Entities

On-granting agreements will be signed between AE, and the national institutions (EE) that will manage and channel the funds to the localities with the feasibility studies: ANFCT, ANFT, FIE and DGS (new Annexes AA). All in country is having on-going experience as leading institutions in channelling resources to local governments (either through mechanisms such as “donor funds” or directly) and are deploying the LoCAL mechanism in case of ANFCT, ANFT, and FIE.

#### Implementing partner

In 2015, BOAD and UNCDF signed a Memorandum of Understanding according to which "the parties commit to work for the identification of local capacities and competencies of local actors for the setting-up of programs of adaptation, to establish jointly and implement adaptation projects in UEMOA countries, drawing on the LoCAL initiative". To ensure that the PBCRGs are implemented in a manner consistent with the LoCAL standard and other attracting domestic and international climate finance, BOAD will sign a Financing Agreement with UNCDF, where UNCDF will provide technical assistance to BOAD. The UNCDF team will be led by Mr. Jean-Pierre Mabiala, who is serving as an Implementing Partner. UNCDF will notably provide expertise and support quality assurance for the design of projects and programmes. The UNCDF team will report directly to BOAD and will inform and provide recommendations to each national technical committee.

#### LoCAL Technical Committees

In line with the LoCAL initiative, a National Technical Committee will provide day-to-day guidance and support to local governments and subnational stakeholders on the implementation and monitoring of programme activities, in line with the LoCAL standard. These committees are co-chaired by the national institutions in charge of local authorities, climate change and adaptation. The committee will be responsible for ensuring transparency, accountability, learning, clear communication, and independent reviews, maintaining transparency and accountability in all decisions.

#### Local Governments

Target communities benefiting from the PBCRGs are the ultimate decision-makers for local adaptation investments, under the oversight of the LoCAL Technical Team. They sign an engagement charter with national institutions to ensure that they have a say on how funds will be used. The LoCAL standard requires that local governments, using communal structures and decentralized state services, are mandated to implement the PBCRGs. Local councils are responsible for planning, budgeting, and managing local investments.

## BOAD (West African Development Bank)

BOAD is the key financial institution for the West African Monetary Union States, promoting balanced development and economic integration. Accredited by the Green Climate Fund (GCF) in 2016, BOAD provides climate change financing to member countries through regional, national, and local guarantees. Its 2021-2025 Strategic Plan, "DÜOLIBA," focuses on regional integration, value creation and job support, and the fight against poverty.

UNICEF through the LOCAL mechanism has been intervening in sub-national adaptation since 2011, with a deployment started in Africa in 2014. The national EEs managing the PBCRG Facilities are the national institutions in each country in charge of the implementation of the GCF projects and activities. They have the responsibility to ensure that the adequate structures and mechanisms are in place to ensure the management of GCF funds.

## UEMOA CCT, an advisory body established in 2011

UEMOA CCT aims to enhance local and regional authorities' involvement in regional integration, promote multi-level governance, and address local development concerns. It supports UEMOA's objectives by providing a platform for dialogue between the national and sub-national levels of government through collaborative actions. The Council, comprising representatives from local and regional authorities, technical experts, and civil society organizations, will be responsible for implementing the GCF projects in UEMOA countries for 2015-2019 and its new Strategic Plan for 2021-2025 ("DÜOLIBA"), and will benefit from BOAD's experience, which provides a solid basis to implement projects that meet the needs of States in alignment with the GCF strategy. The Board will leverage on UNICEF’s and UEMOA’s expertise in PBC development and execution for scale adaptation and dissemination programmes.

## B.S. Justification for GCF funding request (max 1000 words, approximately two pages)


---
**Page 23**
---

The importance of the sub-national level in climate adaptation is recognized in the Paris Agreement (Articles 7 and 11). However, local governments and communities in Least Developed Countries (LDCs) and other developing nations often face significant challenges in accessing resources needed to continue their adaptation efforts. These challenges are exacerbated by factors such as SMES, NGOs, producers' associations, and cooperatives also facing constraints in accessing the resources needed to continue to implement their climate change efforts. This program aims to solve a paradigm shift on the target at the largest fleet of countries committed with both-concurrent and sequential approaches, addressing climate underfunding in local adaptation investments.

Despite the Paris Agreement's commitment to mobilize USD 100 billion annually by 2020, adaptation finance remains between 20-25% of committed concessional financing and has shown little movement. Less than a quarter of all resources are channeled to reach LDCs and other developing countries (ODCs), and adaptation finance is increasing at a lower rate than adaptation needs, widening the adaptation gap in these countries. In contrast, developed countries are estimated to reach USD 140–300 billion by 2020 and USD 280–600 billion by 2050.

The climate change impacts on LDCs and ODCs are more severe than those of developed countries because they rely heavily on natural resource bases and high public debt. Their public debt levels have doubled over the last decade, and while none are currently at risk of defaulting, their fiscal space is constrained. Climate change economics suggest that climate change will cost LDCs USD 78.9 billion for climate adaptation in 2015. The average ODI share of total spending on climate change is 3%, with high-income countries spending more than double the amount spent by low-income countries.

In many LDCs and ODCs, adaptation investments are heavily underfunded. These countries list billions of dollars needed for adaptation investments, yet face increasing vulnerabilities and macroeconomic challenges that threaten their ability to finance these investments. In addition, most LDCs can only burden the budget with a limited amount of resources because investments do not typically generate returns for development but rather provide public goods and services.

In many LDCs and ODCs, adaptation investment ratios further decrease, and viable mechanisms to channel climate finance to the local level are limited. National funds exist but are not fully dedicated to adaptation financing for subnational actors. The endowment of national emission funds in Côte d'Ivoire and the existing funds in Mali, Niger, and Burkina Faso are examples of how these countries need scaling up.

Given the low levels of resources available to local governments, they are not in a position to finance climate-specific investments at scale. In addition, the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and other multilateral development banks (MDBs) and Based Climate Resilience Grants aim to cover additional costs for making investments climate resilient, addressing the financing gap for local resilience-building priorities.

In contrast, the Caribbean region has shown improvements in financial inclusion, but adaptation investments are still perceived as too risky for underdeveloped domestic financial sectors. The program will support a wide range of subnational actors in accessing finance at both national and sectoral plans but currently hindered by capacity barriers, especially at the subnational level. The program will support a wide range of subnational actors in accessing finance at both national and sectoral plans but currently hindered by capacity barriers, especially at the subnational level.

The program will also contribute to GCF's Adaptation Investment Criteria and provide integration of national climate strategies in the target countries.

GCF is not necessary to structure and implement a multi-instrument approach, supporting both the enabling environment and the mobilization of additional resources for scaling up the program. In the long term, the program will facilitate access to finance by subnational actors through the use of blended financing instruments where local actors are put in place. For instance, in Cambodia, the PICBRC system was used to channel additional funding from other donors after the Local Climate mechanism was rolled out.

From a policy perspective, the pilot projects have demonstrated their relevance and feasibility, motivating governments to scale up the approach nationally. BAOAD aims to implement a long-term regional program leveraging the National Finance Facilitators for direct financing and channeled private sector investments. These facilities will serve as loss-limited capital or provide seed capital to unlock commercial capital or provide demonstration effects.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 20

Relevant countries in the four countries have endorsed the LocCAL approach and BOAD's blended finance instruments used to begin work with the GCF and invested entities. Proposed investments in rural and semi-urban areas are underpinned by a strong focus on climate change adaptation, including through the use of green bonds and repayable grants through Blended Finance Facilities to de-risk private sector projects and finance public climate-proof investments.

The economic analysis (see Annex 3) underscores the financial non-volatility of some locally prioritized adaptation interventions, which, however, bring significant social, economic, and environmental benefits. This justifies the need for a blended financing approach that combines concessional funding from GCF with market-based knowledge generation, enhanced local climate governance, and climate-resilient investments realized through GCF funding.

## B.5. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

To ensure the long-term sustainability of the financing mechanisms and facilities in their largest countries, the programme will support the development of a national LocCAL mechanism that can be scaled up to cover other sectors such as decentralization and climate change governance. It strengthens these elements to achieve adaptation climate-proof investments. The programme also supports the institutionalization of the LocCAL mechanism through capacity building, technical assistance (TA), and institutionalized financing mechanisms; the programme adds an implementation phase beyond the project's end.

The different institutions involved in implementing the Local mechanism in each country will be notably supported for the systematic adoption and operationalization of specific gender-sensitive features into national systems for budgeting, planning, and decision-making. The programme also supports the development of a national body in charge of implementation of gender norms – notably through involvement in LocCAL Committees. The programme also supports the institutionalization of the LocCAL mechanism by supporting the government in participating countries through policy, regulatory or institutional reforms, thus ensuring proper national appropriation.

The programme aims to mainstream gender-sensitive climate change adaptation into regular development funding at the national level and to ensure that the GCF's investments are aligned with the country's NDCs. This will be achieved through capacity building and TA for local actors to integrate climate change adaptation into project design and implementation, including through the use of blended finance instruments such as PBRCD and Blended Finance Facilities. The gradual institutionalization and overall ownership of the PBRCD system by the participating countries will ensure that the programme's investments are sustainable beyond the project's end. The Blended Finance Facilities will address market and non-market barriers to private sector mobilization of climate funds.

The programme follows a phased approach of the LocCAL mechanism:

* **Phase I: Test.** The aim is to test the mechanism in a small number of pilot projects (e.g., 10-20 investment cycles) in the four countries, with the first phase being set up in Burkina Faso and Côte d'Ivoire.
* **Phase II: Consolidate.** This phase integrates the lessons of the first phase. It is deployed to at least 5-10 local governments in each country.
* **Phase III: Systematize.** This phase consists of progressively covering all vulnerable local governments and ultimately the entire national territory.

Examples from other countries illustrate the sustainability of this approach. In Bhutan, the PRECITY initiative covered two gewogs and expanded to 100 gewags as part of a national rollout, with investments focusing on integrated water resources management (IWRM) and climate change adaptation. The programme has been deployed in up to 50 provinces and districts, channeling climate finance for local adaptation investments through partnerships with various donors.

The blended financing mechanism will provide grants on a revolving basis, expected to remain operational for 10 years and become self-sufficient after the initial capital injection. Re-capitalization through new capital shares and loan guarantees will be required as needed. The programme will support sound management practices, and cash flow monitoring will be implemented to maintain self-sustainability.

Partner financial organizations can propose additional financing instruments, such as preferential loans or guarantees, to de-risk private sector investments. In some cases, we may also consider the use of other Blended Finance Facilities, ensuring their long-term sustainability beyond the initial subsidized phases. Institutional processes, including capacity building and technical assistance (TA), will be provided for the development of a national LocCAL mechanism.

Throughout the programme's implementation, emphasis will be placed on mobilizing international and domestic resources for subnational adaptation finance. The programme will support advocacy initiatives by the LOCAL Global Board and member countries, promoting their contributions to the establishment of internationally recognized standard mechanisms for subnational adaptation finance.

The programme includes sensitization and capacity building at the sub-national and national levels and through the implementation of pilot projects in selected countries, which will empower a wide range of local actors with the knowledge, know-how, tools and agency to act as adaptation champions.

The programme mainstreams a "learning by doing" approach, as the whole deployment of the financing instruments is based on the identification of concrete adaptation actions at the country level, especially the local one. Subsidary agreements will be signed between the AE and country level EEIs to strengthen their capacity in the implementation of the GCF projects. The programme also aims to support sub-national planning, budgeting and execution and monitoring processes. Local governments and development partners are supported through a series of training workshops, seminars, webinars, international training and exchange through UNCDF and the LoCAL global network. Local communities are in turn sensitized through local support teams and their awareness and agency enhanced. The programme leverages the experience gained from the pilot projects to develop a replicable model that can be scaled up toward local governments and actors – ensuring sustainability of capacity building processes and outcomes.


---
**Page 29**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 25

Technological innovation is assured through the menus of eligible investments (see Annexes 2). The menus align to national climate change policies and frameworks (NDCs, NAPs, etc.) and feed from climate risks analysis to localize adaptation responses. They will also be informed by lessons learned from successful innovations and best practices tested in each country and in the region for climate sensitive sectors and will guide local governments in identifying innovative adaptation responses.

The proposal aims at supporting the development of new tools and use of climate data and information innovative tools, e.g., through its climate risk analysis methodology and the LISA. Similarly, community and local-level actors are supported to develop and test digital solutions for payments (e.g., mobile money), contributing, on one side, to improve transparency in public procurement processes, under local governments' contracts, and, on the other side, to boost the financial inclusion of local communities.

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section reflects the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.

### D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The programme will contribute to reducing climate resilience of communities and environments across four countries, impacting livelihoods through gradual deployment of financing mechanisms. Targeting 85 communities with an estimated population of over 234,000 people, the programme will provide a range of services and support for GCF-funded investments by its completion, while all residents will indirectly benefit from capacity building and specialized technical assistance.

The PBCRGS will support approximately 530 local government-led investments in priority climate-sensitive sectors, providing EUR1,135,200 in direct financing to local governments. Additionally, the Blended Finance Facility will offer EUR487,600 in co-financing for these investments. The programme will also provide technical assistance and capacity needs through participatory and gender-sensitive planning processes. All outputs described in this programme will serve to meet the GCF’s investment criteria.

#### Investment in planning and governance processes
- Access to resilient infrastructure and services
- Technical assistance to local governments
- Participatory and gender-sensitive infrastructure management
- Access to financial products for private sector adaptation investments

More broadly, the following will accrue to indirect beneficiaries:
- Local economic opportunities generated by investments and,
- Infrastructure and service delivery improvements.
- Improved sub-national planning and PFM at the local level.

PBCRGS-related investments will include facilitating participatory environmental and social assessments, and the construction of climate-smart infrastructure. Vulnerable communities and groups will participate in decision-making and implementation processes, ensuring investments meet their needs and generate economic and social benefits. The programme will also provide technical assistance to build capacity for local SMEs and producers organizations, enhancing the adaptive capacity of target communities.

The programme will contribute to GCF’s Adaptation Results Areas through investments in seven priority sectors, including communities with infrastructure, products, and services to cope with climate change impacts. Examples include:
- Vulnerable livelihoods (AHA 1.0)
- Health and well-being, food security and water resources (AHA 2.0)
- Climate resistant infrastructure and built environment (AHA 3.0)
- Provenance of natural resources (AHA 4.0)

Adaptation benefits and impacts will be evaluated using the ACCAE M&E framework through local governments. This will involve collecting baseline data on hazards, vulnerabilities, and climate risks to track investments and monitor progress towards adaptation results areas. The programme will also provide technical assistance for GCF and programme outcomes, providing insights to strengthen subnational adaptation finance and action.

Overall, the programme will mobilize international and domestic resources, ensuring the ownership and credibility of local central and local governments in adaptation planning and implementation. It will also promote advocacy by the Local Global Fund and member countries, promoting good practices and establishing internationally recognized standards for subnational adaptation finance.


### D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)


---
**Page 31**

# Potential for scale-up and replication

The programme aims specifically to continue rolling out LoCAL in Burkina Faso, Mali and Niger and to introduce the approach in Ivory Coast (Component 2.1), while testing and deploying an additional financing model as the Blended Finance Facility (Component 2.2). The blended finance facility will be a flexible, scalable, and self-sufficient mechanism that can be replicated after the programme. These mechanisms are designed to be flexible, scalable, and self-sufficient so they gain proof of concept in four countries and can then be scaled up.

Operating in four countries will provide economies of scale, generate and test tools with high replication potential, particularly in UEMOA countries, and accelerate financing models for local climate finance deployment.

## Programme implementation

A Regional Programme Board will be established to ensure the four target countries to share experiences, lessons learned, and best practices. These countries will join the LiCoAL network within the LoCAL Global Board. The LoCAL programme will also establish a regional knowledge management platform that will support the sharing of information on climate finance mechanisms and locally adapted solutions.

The regional PMU will coordinate with countries to ensure effective outreach and knowledge sharing among stakeholders, including national governments, local authorities, civil society organizations (CSOs), and private sector actors. The blended finance facility as well as the LoCAL programme will be sustainable models for subnational climate finance. The programme benefits from a series of existing regional platforms that support the exchange of information on climate change adaptation and mitigation.

Continuous learning process is supported by knowledge sharing and South-South cooperation. The programme will continue to benefit from these insights, as documented in the local mid-term evaluation and publications, recognized by the LoCAL Global Board.

## Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

The programme will establish an institutional mechanism for channeling climate finance to local governments (Component 2.1) through a dedicated financing facility with locally dedicated adaptation funding for total private sector actors, such as SMEs and producers' organizations (Component 2.2). It will enhance the capacities of local government institutions to integrate climate change into their planning processes, improve public participation in decision-making processes, and improve community response capacities through sensitization and awareness (Component 1). This will help countries to better manage risks associated with climate change, reduce exposure to climate-related risks and reinforce multi-level governance for climate adaptation. Additionally, by demonstrating the effectiveness of the financing mechanisms, the programme aims to attract further domestic and international funding, thereby increasing the overall scale-up potential.

## Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

Integrating climate change adaptation into national and local government processes will institutionalize and mainstream adaptation within decentralization systems in each country, ensuring long-term and sustainable use of the programme. The LoCAL programme will support countries in developing or revising their national adaptation plans (NAPs) with central and local governments and other stakeholders. In the long term, the programme supports the enhancement of national climate change policies and strategies by supporting the development of a national framework for climate change budget tagging, developing or revising guidelines and manuals, integrating adaptation into total planning guidance documents, and strengthening institutions that handle private sector financing to create dedicated adaptation windows, benefiting a broader range of local private sector actors.

## Overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways consistent with relevant national climate change priorities and plans

The programme facilitates the translation of Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) adaptation priorities into local actions. Eligible adaptation investment menus are aligned with NDCs and National Adaptation Plans (NAPs) while ensuring that they are locally owned, demand-driven, and based on participatory processes. The LoCAL programme is autonomous in adaptation efforts by involving them in the entire process, from risk-informed planning to the selection, implementation, and monitoring of investments. Drawing from past experiences, local interventions often expand from

# D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

This programme presents an integrated approach which contributes to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and aligns actions in an environmentally, socially and economically sustainable manner.

The programme will lead to the implementation of both public and private investment applications of different types through a multi- and cross-sectoral approach. A series of co-benefits are expected:

* **Economic co-benefits** include boosting more dynamic and green local economies, improving local conditions for employment and entrepreneurship (including micro-enterprises, small and medium enterprises), and/or local SMEs. The increased resilience of economic infrastructure and services will have spin-off effects over different communities and sectors. This will reduce the vulnerability of people to climate change impacts such as crop disruptions during rainy seasons and overall improve mobility of people and goods in the area. Similarly, Climate proofed investments in agriculture and livestock will contribute to increasing agricultural productivity while making agricultural income less vulnerable to the effects of climate change. Through local contracting and/or community contracts, the programme will also provide opportunities for women and youth to participate in the economy and generate new income prospects for local communities.

* **Social co-benefits** include improving local governance on climate change issues and in general, such as environmental management, disaster risk reduction, public participation, planning, implementation, monitoring and management of local investments, while increasing accountability and transparency of public servants on local (adaptation) actions. The programme will also contribute to the empowerment of women and youth through increased access and voices of most vulnerable groups, such as women and youths. GCF-funded investments support sustainable development in communities by ensuring that all project activities are aligned with the SDGs and the national priorities aligned with the SDGs, with the goal of being inclusive, sustainable and leaving no one behind. Key areas of intervention include: (i) climate change adaptation; (ii) climate change mitigation; (iii) co-benefits for development benefits; development benefits in particularly deprived areas, mostly through access to improved social basic services such as water supply, sanitation, energy, health care, education and transport; and (iv) co-benefits related to project design and execution, including pricing products and services with public good features at affordable levels.

* **Environmental co-benefits** include increasing awareness on climate change and maximizing mitigation co-benefits toward a net zero carbon development. The menu of eligible investments encourages the realization of ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) measures in line with the GCF’s strategic priorities, including water resources management, reforestation), of environmental sound agricultural practices and use of renewables

## Key Assumptions and Methodology

### Energy Sector
The energy sector is often neglected when applicable, and seeks co-benefits in terms of sustainable land use and greenhouse gas sequestration (e.g., afforestation and/or wetlands conservation). The overall sensitization efforts with communities will contribute to the awareness raising of women and youth, leading to inducing behaviour changes which are more conscious of human impacts on the natural ecosystems.

### Gender Mainstreaming
Gender mainstreaming translates into taking account the specific vulnerabilities of women in climate risk analysis and decision-making. It also involves ensuring that gender equality is considered throughout the design, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and management of investments. Gender issues and development impact will be described in the gender-disaggregated baseline data and in the project-specific gender action plan (GAP) for each country.

### D.A. Needs of Recipients
D.A. needs of recipients (max. 300 words approximately) 1 page

As part of the preparation and implementation of the NDCs, the key areas of vulnerability to climate change and the priorities for intervention have been defined. This programme aligns with the four countries' targets as expressed in their NDCs, which include addressing terrestrial sectoral measures to be addressed to specific vulnerabilities faced by target communities.

All four countries are disproportionately affected by the negative effects of climate change, and their vulnerabilities to climate change are exacerbated by existing socio-economic conditions.

### Key Obstacles
Key obstacles observed in the target countries in sub-national effective climate response pertain to both governance and enabling environment, operational risks and barriers at project level:

- Lack of appropriate budgetary allocations from the national level for climate-sensitive sectors.
- Most of the climate change adaptation projects are not aligned with existing national programmes that have specific earmarked arrangements and which fall outside established decision-making processes and the public financial management systems.
- Constraints and lack in terms of access to adaptation finance for private sector-led investments.
- Lack of a clear institutional framework in terms of governance, accountability, and oversight to drive a climate risk-informed development planning.

### NDC Gap Assessment
Overall, there is a need to encompass both technical and financing constraints to implement both public and private local investments that respond to communities' adaptation needs beyond business as usual development pathways.

### NDC Gap Requirements Assessed at Adaptation
- Burkina Faso: NDC anticipates a total of approx. 28.6 billion USD to meet the country's adaptation priority needs (conditional/ unconditional scenario).
- Chad: The total cost of adaptation actions for the period 2020-2030 is estimated at 12 billion USD.
- Mali: The total cost of adaptation measures for the period 2020-2030 is estimated at 8 billion USD.
- Niger: The total cost of adaptation actions for the period 2020-2030 is estimated at 4.6 billion USD (total US$ 6.743 billion).

### Priority Issues for Countries

* **Climate change is particularly impacting in a context where countries’ development depends on access to natural resources and climate conditions.**

    * Identification of adaptation measures, including helping countries to identify priority areas for investment and to develop national action plans.

    * Realization of localized and spatialized analyses aiming to characterize the vulnerability of local communities, ecosystems, and economic sectors to climate change.

### D.S. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The four countries have provided their non-objection letters, which are attached to this proposal. The programme is envisaged as a contribution to the implementation of the National Climate Change Strategies and Plans for climate change and decentralization and is aligned with NDCs. The design studies of the national mechanisms in each country reflect the specificities of these countries’ contexts: political framework, institutional framework, complementaries with initiatives in place or in preparation.

In Burkina Faso, local governments follow the Yamoussoukro Declaration (2015), emphasizing the need for the decentralization process to be accompanied by a strengthening of the capacity of local authorities and increased mobilization of municipalities for climate actions, aligning with the National Adaptation Plan’s goal of enhancing the resilience of communities. The country has also adopted the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development (Agenda 2030) and the Paris Agreement. Burkina Faso is a signatory to the “Lhorhore 2840” highlights significant local resources and capacities despite efforts to strengthen decentralization (see adoption of PNDES + in 2021-2025). LUCAL supports inclusive and resilient local development, thus aligning with Burkina Faso’s National Development Plan (PND) (2018), the Sustainable Development Strategy (SDS) (2017), the National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) (2013), and the Low-carbon Development Vision 2050 (2022).

In Côte d’Ivoire, the country has a robust institutional framework for national climate governance, including the National Climate Change Programme, created in 2012, which coordinates climate-related projects and initiatives. A Directive on the fight against Climate Change was adopted by the Government of Côte d'Ivoire in 2015. The NDCs are aligned with the country’s development objectives in local development plans and strategies. The framework supports dialogue and action on climate issues, including through the National Committee for Climate Change (CNCC) and the national NDC focal point.

In Mali, LUCAL aligns with the National Policy on Climate Change (PNPC), which enshrines integrating climate change into national and local development planning. LUCAL also supports the Framework Document for National Decentralization Policy (PDCDN), (2015-2024) and the Strategic Framework for Economic Recovery and Sustainable Development (SFERSD) (2018). The country has adopted the NDCs, focusing on poverty reduction, environmental impact, and enhancing human capital, especially for youth and women. In Mali, LUCAL supports inclusive and resilient local development, thus aligning with the National Climate Change Strategy and international cooperation. LUCAL also aligns with Mali’s revised NDC (2021), which promotes local authorities’ involvement in planning through their Social, Economic, and Cultural Development Plans (PSEC).


---
**Page 35**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 31

In Niger, the National Climate Change Policy (NCCP, 2013) seeks to integrate climate change into local planning. Meanwhile, the Economic and Social Development Plan (PDES 2017-2021) focuses on eradicating poverty and improving living conditions in rural areas. The PDES is being replaced by the PDES with the Resilience Program for the Safeguarding of the Homeland (PSR), developed around five strategic axes: human capital, governance, peacebuilding, social inclusion, and environment. Local actors (LACs) are at the center of this program, which uses the "3N" model for food security and sustainable agriculture, emphasizing local actor roles in poverty reduction and sustainable resource management, while the National Climate Change Policy is being implemented.

This programme has ensured an early and strategic engagement with national authorities and other government stakeholders to support national ownership. Annexes 7 detail past and forthcoming activities.


## D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max: 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The PBCRG system is expected to be more efficient in mobilizing climate finance due to the geographic location and targeted local actors. About 70% of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) funding will be allocated for investments through the PSR, with the remaining 30% for other projects. The GCF's financial mechanism for supporting LACs is provided via result-based payments, which offer liquidity for investments and establish an accountability system to ensure that funds are used effectively.

In the short term, the programme aims to secure additional resources through capitalization of outcomes and direct support from national governments. This approach has been demonstrated in Cambodia where multiple donors have allocated PBCRG to fund climate-resilient infrastructure. Over time, however, funding is expected to decrease, with national governments directly co-funding the PSR (i.e., Of Benin through ecotourism).

In the long term, the programme will focus on mobilizing domestic financing from central and local governments, ensuring that resources are used effectively for climate resilience measures. The PBCRG system has been demonstrated in Cambodia where multiple donors have allocated PBCRG to fund climate-resilient infrastructure. Over time, however, funding is expected to decrease, with national governments directly co-funding the PSR (i.e., Of Benin through ecotourism).

Key points from the LAC initiative, which informs this programme, highlight the effectiveness of the PBCRG system in mobilizing resources for climate resilience measures. The LAC initiative has demonstrated its ability to create parallel systems, facilitate scaling, and promote national ownership. Performance measures reinforce local governance and accountability, while the LAC initiative's experience with the Local Climate Adaptation (LCA) initiative enhances transparency and accountability, verifying climate change expenditure at the local level.

The proposed financing mechanisms capitalize on existing national and sub-national institutions and systems, reducing transaction costs for both public and private actors. The PBCRG system is designed to be flexible and scalable, guaranteeing country ownership and effective scaling up with the LAC mechanism already proven across 18 countries in Africa. This flexibility ensures that climate resilience measures can be effectively implemented at scale and integrated into the programme, enhancing the PBCRG system's effectiveness.

The proposed financing mechanisms also aim to leverage existing national and sub-national institutions for local climate action, reviving revenue-generating investments that can be more efficiently financed through de-risking or blended finance instruments. This approach optimizes the use of scarce public resources for adaptation, ensuring the sustainable implementation of climate resilience measures at the sub-national level.

### E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)
- A common monitoring and evaluation framework will be designed and will be aligned with the Assessing Climate Change Adaptation Framework (ACADF) developed by GCF.
- The ACADF is a tool to assess the climate change adaptation performance of each country using three components related to the technical design elements of the mechanism in each country and relate to the deployment of the PBCRG system from year-to-year. The ACADF will be handled on an annual basis, with the first assessment being conducted at the end of 2019.
- The ACADF will contribute for an advancement of the programme's aspiration objectives and demonstrate its contribution through the financing mechanisms deployed. The programme M&E system will be used to monitor the progress towards achieving these objectives.

### Continuous monitoring of the implementation of the programme
- Will be carried out under the responsibility of the regional oversight bodies (Regional PIs) in close cooperation with the local decision-making bodies (LUCAD, Technical Committees, Blended Finance Facilities Committee, EEs, and local governments).
- At the level of each country, annual performance assessments of the target national government will be carried out - neutrality will be ensured from the pre-assessment stage to the final report.
- Each report will provide an account of the implementation of the activities, the difficulties encountered, the lessons learned, as well as the degree of achievement of the objectives measured by the indicators, referring to the logical framework matrix. The report will be designed to allow a follow-up of the means envisaged for the implementation of the programme and the results obtained.
- An evaluation report will be produced at the end of the programme as well as mid-term - in order to take into account possible warning points and adjustments that may be necessary, taking into consideration the lessons learned from the annual performance assessments. The ACADF will be used to monitor progress towards achieving the objectives set out in the logical framework matrix.
- Reports will be shared with programme partners; a transversal evaluation report will be produced at the end of the programme.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.I. Risk and mitigation measures (see: 3 pages)

In Mali, Niger, Burkina Faso, the programme builds on a successful LOCAL pilot phase, which overall limits financial and operational risks. In each four target countries, risks were assessed as part of the design and (see annexes) throughout implementation.

### Selected Risk Factor 1 – Organizational

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

#### Description
Staff changes (technical) within national and subnational institutions disrupt the implementation of activities

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
National technical teams and request field officers work with government partners in case adjustments are needed in the event of staff changes. By emphasizing the importance of well-established and refined financing mechanisms and individual capacity, any change in personnel should not unduly affect country initiatives. As the LOCAL mechanism is standardized (see annexes I) and subsequent deployment (phase II / III), the risk gradually decreases.

### Selected Risk Factor 2 – Governance

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | High |

#### Description
Abuse of funds by public servants (mismanagement, corruption or "clientelism")

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
Capacity building emphasizes the ability of government officials to use government systems properly to avoid misappropriation of funds. These exercises identify any misappropriation of funds as soon as possible. Financial reports require a detailed explanation for all expenditures and are subject to regular audits by the auditor general or other bodies of funds. The results of good governance and verification are integrated into the design of the PBCHD system (in the form of performance indicators, including grants at the local level each year).

### Selected Risk Factor 3 – Low Impact

#### Description
The projects identified and implemented do not ultimately contribute to improving climate resilience.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
The menu of eligible investments is defined during the design of the mechanism in each country, in close collaboration with government partners. The menu includes a range of interventions that are consistent with the results of field- and spatialized assessments of climate risks. When expanding to new local governments (phase II), the menu will be updated to include new components as part of the programme.

### Selected Risk Factor 4 – Financial Management

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

#### Description


---
**Page 50**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 46

## Maintenance of investments is not done.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Investment maintenance funding through other own income or other transfers are tracked and documented on an annual basis during annual performance assessment and rewarded by the scoring system. The participation of local communities in the project implementation and monitoring phase helps mitigate risk.

## Selected Risk Factor 5 - Operational delays

### Category
Technical and operational

### Probability
Medium

### Impact
High Select

### Description
Delays occur due to implementation delays and lack of co-funding from the government and/or other partners. 

### Mitigation Measure(s)
National technical teams and expert field officers provide day-to-day support to local governments to ensure timely and high-quality project implementation. The PMU also provides training for a community-based team, including national and international technical assistance and training. The Regional PMU supports country documents by experience lessons, exposure visits, and other forms of technical assistance. The PMU will continue to provide capacity building through various mechanisms, particularly in phase I. Regular dialogue is maintained with development partners through working groups and regular meetings. Support is provided to develop complementary support programmes that respond to country priorities.

## Selected Risk Factor 6 - Environmental

### Category
Technical and operational

### Probability
Low

### Impact
Medium

### Description
Projects implemented have negative environmental and/or social consequences.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Ecosystems and socio-economic vulnerabilities are included in localised and specialized analysis of climate risks. Environmental protection measures and incentives for participation in planning, implementation and monitoring are incorporated into the project design. The PMU will provide technical assistance to countries to ensure that they have a national level EIA and/or performance measures influencing the score and finance allocation of the following year). As countries move towards more advanced levels of environmental management, the GCF will consider introducing an additional mechanism or (e.g. green job creation) at GCF financing investments will undergo a mandatory ESS according to relevant laws and regulations. The PMU will provide technical assistance funding through the SAI mechanism to support the implementation of the programme.

## Selected Risk Factor 7 - Security

### Category
Other

### Probability
Low

### Impact
Medium

### Description
Côte d'Ivoire is relatively politically stable. However, in Burkina Faso and Niger face political instability, posing potential risks to the proposed project. Some areas are medium security risk areas - with armed groups and/or terrorist groups present. The PMU will provide technical assistance to countries to ensure that they have a national level of the mechanism and the mobilization of technical assistance (e.g. endangering support teams, annual performance assessment). The PMU will also conduct an analysis of each of these countries political/security situation and detailed mitigating measures is provided in annex E.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The programme intends to mitigate potential security risks through various strategies (please refer to Conflict sensitivity analysis):  
- Prioritizing stable regions: Selecting project sites with minimal security concerns and a history of peacebuilding efforts.

Engaging with local stakeholders: Collaborating with local law enforcement, community leaders, and traditional authorities to understand local dynamics and build trust.

Establishing a grievance redressal mechanism for inclusive project development and resource allocation to prevent grievances and foster social cohesion.

Employing a conflict resolution mechanism: Respecting traditional practices while incorporating innovative approaches to address potential conflicts arising from the project.

Implementing risk assessments, establishing emergency response procedures, and providing security training for project personnel.
