F. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The environmental and social risk assessment for this program aligns with internationally recognized standards from the IFC, World Bank, and GCF, and adheres to BOAD's policies. For detailed guidelines, see Annex 6 of ESS.

The assessment includes:

- A Checklist for Category C - An "Environmental and Social Screening Checklist™" has been developed according to GCF procedures for Category C projects; please refer to Annex 6A1.
- Environmental and Social Management Framework (Annex 6A2)
- Indigenous People Plan
- Cultural Heritage Plan
- BOAD's General Guidelines on ESS

In accordance with the "Operational Policies and Procedures of West African Development Bank Intervention for Environment and Social Assessment" and the Financing Programs, "EAS risks and impacts are assessed based on regulations of the intervention countries and guidelines from international organizations, including: (i) United Nations Convention to Combat Desertification; (ii) United Nations Declaration on the Elimination of一切形式的种族歧视; (iii) United Nations Universal Declaration of Human Rights; (iv) International Labor Organization's Conventions 182 and 190; (v) United Nations Convention on Biological Diversity; (vi) IFC GCF policies on safeguards on environment and social issues; and (vii) international good industry practices published by major industry associations. In particular, the following environmental risks and impacts will be taken into account:

- Environmental risks: Risks to biodiversity, including species extinction or endangerment, loss of habitat, and impacts and any real threat to the protection, conservation, preservation and restoration of natural habitats and landscapes; risks related to water resources.
- Social risks and impacts: Human security threats from conflicts, crime, or violence; disproportionate impacts on vulnerable groups such as women, children, indigenous peoples, and people with disabilities.

The following environmental and social screening checklist is used for the environmental risk assessment for the program's environmental and social risks. All proposed adaptation interventions and related activities have been pre-screened and categorized under Category C according to the GCF Guidelines™, which covers activities such as:

- Awareness raising, capacity building, advisory services, support for adaptation planning, technical support, and information dissemination.
- Household-level facilities and production within built-up areas, including post-harvest processing, rainwater harvesting, water supply and sanitation, and solid waste management.
- Small-scale rural and urban projects, including village-level water supply and drainage, rural energy, small-scale infrastructure development (e.g., roads, bridges), land rehabilitation, climate-resilient agriculture, soil and water conservation, and community forest management.

Please refer to Annex 2 for the menu of eligible investments for the adaptation interventions for each individual category. The proposed adaptation interventions have been pre-screened according to the GCF Guidelines™ and are categorized under Category C according to the GCF Guidelines™ and are not required to undergo a formal ESS process at this stage.

However, as part of the project information for the adaptation interventions is being developed, including detailed project area, all proposed adaptation interventions and related activities will undergo a second internal mandatory ESS assessment in accordance with BOAD ESS and ESM procedures before final selection for direct funding from PBCRS. A checklist annex is attached.

1) To assess this checklist, we have referred to Annex 1, Chapter on Part B ESS Screening of the "Guidelines for the environmental and social screening of activities proposed under the SAC" by the Project Board of the West African Development Bank.
2) GCF. Sustainability Guidance Note: Screening and Categorizing GCF-Financed Activities (2019)

### G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The LoCAL programme in Mali, Niger, Burkina Faso, and Côte d’Ivoire integrates a gender-sensitive approach to climate change adaptation by considering the different impacts of climate change on women and men as well as by climate change. Gender assessments and an action plan, prepared by gender experts, guide this approach. The assessment focuses on women’s rights and status, climate and gender issues, and specific gender challenges for the programme.

Despite government efforts to integrate gender into public policies, significant gaps remain in application and resource allocation. These gaps are particularly evident when it comes to addressing the needs of women and girls with regard to gender inequality issues, highlighting the need for effective gender mainstreaming. Detailed gender assessments for the LoCAL projects will be conducted by gender experts.

#### Main recommendations from Gender Assessments:
- Improve local-level data on gender inequalities, using indicators linked to Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and other relevant indicators.
- Conduct studies on the co-benefits of climate change, gender-focused adaptation.
- Improve women’s access to resources and services.
- Facilitate women’s professionalization through initiatives like FIRCA in Côte d’Ivoire’s agricultural sector.

#### Gender Action Plan Implementation:
- Systematizing gender analysis in vulnerability assessments and activity reports.
- Facilitating women’s participation by ensuring their representation in local adaptation plans and investment choices.
- Incorporating gender criteria in performance reviews.

### Key Dimensions for Gender Vulnerability
- **Gender-Specific Vulnerabilities:** Adaptation responses will consider gender-specific vulnerabilities and barriers to climate change adaptation.
- **Inclusive Civic Space:** Awareness and capacity building will address the needs of vulnerable groups, including women and girls.
- **Participatory Planning:** The LoCAL mechanism promotes participatory and gender-sensitive planning, identifying and addressing the needs of the most vulnerable and advancing gender equality.

### Design Factors for Gender Sensitivity
- **Climate Risk Assessments:** Methodologies account for local stakeholder group-specific vulnerabilities.


---
**Page 54**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 50

* **Capacity Building:** Training and technical assistance will integrate gender mainstreaming throughout the project management cycle.
* **Gender-Responsive Investments:** A menu of eligible investments will target areas where women's empowerment is a key driver for climate change adaptation.
* **Performance Measures:** Local governments will be assessed annually on gender-responsive adaptation actions, with results reported to the GCF Board.
* **M&E Tools:** The ACAC methodology tracks adaptation benefits distribution, assessing improvements in women's access to resources and services.

### Blended Finance Facilities:

The Blended Finance Facilities will develop investment policies ensuring contributions to gender equality and women's empowerment are integrated into loan terms and policies to integrate gender considerations in project origination and financing.

### Investment Screening:

Investments will be screened for climate rationale, considering impacts on vulnerable populations, including women. A gender analysis will be conducted as part of the due diligence process.

### Safeguarding Policies:

Policies on Sexual Exploitation, Abuse, and Harassment (SEAH) will be integrated into the Environmental and Social Safeguards Framework. These policies will apply to GCF-financed activities, adhering to international safeguards throughout the programme execution.

### Monitoring and Evaluation:

Most indicators will be disaggregated by gender, with specific attention to social inclusion dimensions. The national focal points will include organizations representing women's interests to secure the implementation of the Gender Action Plan.

#### G.3 Financial management and procurement (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Financial management and procurement under this project will follow BOAD rules, regulations, and the Accreditation Memorandum (AM) with GCF. Grant proceeds will flow from GCF to the AM for use then to fund activities based on the Procurement Plan.

### PROCUREMENT

To ensure financing aligns with BOAD’s mandate and maximizes development effectiveness, the Bank seeks to adopt a more efficient procurement system. This project will apply BOAD's guidelines for procuring consultancy services and awarding contracts for works, goods, and non-consultancy services.

BOAD will review procurement films and provide approvals. AMI-due flight clearance will be conducted as part of BOAD’s Financial Reporting and Auditing Project (FRAP). The Bank will also seek pre-clearance from the UN, OECD, FATF, and the WAMIC Directive. It requires disclosing suspicious transactions to authorities and submitting annual reports.

The policy mandates that project funds must not originate from illicit activities and require immediate reporting of any suspicions of unlawful funds. BOAD prohibits business relationships with entities on international sanctions lists related to money laundering and terrorist financing.

### AUDIT

Annual audits will follow BOAD Guidelines for Financial Reporting and Auditing of Projects. An independent external auditor, approved by BOAD, will audit the Financial Statements according to international standards. BOAD may provide technical assistance in preparing financial statements and conducting internal controls.

The audit cost will be covered by project resources. BOAD will ensure KYC-compliant due diligence, including anti-money laundering measures.

As an implementing Partner, UNCDF will manage financial and procurement processes per its policies and BOAD guidelines. UNCDF-funded projects will adhere to the Financial Regulations, Rules, and Internal Control Framework, mandating competitive procurement through formal tenders. Procurement principles include:

F

* **Best Use of Money:** Selecting offers that best meet end-users' needs and provide the best return on investment, considering quality, experience, vendor reputation, life-cycle costs, and alignment with social, environmental, and economic goals.

* **Fairness, Integrity, and Transparency:** Ensuring fair, open, and rules-based competitive processes. All vendors are treated equally, with clear evaluation criteria, instructions, requirements, and easy-to-understand rules.

* **Ethical Conduct:** Ensuring that all parties involved in the selection process act ethically by providing equal participation opportunities, and restricting competition only when necessary to achieve development goals.

In summary, AE will ensure that all financial management, procurement, and due diligence processes align with international standards and best practices, ensuring transparency, fairness, and compliance with all relevant regulations. For full details, please refer to Annex 9 and the relevant sections.

# INTRODUCTION

Une mission d'évaluation du Programme Régional de Financement de l'Adaptation au Niveau local (LoCAL) en Afrique du Ouest (Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Mali et Niger) a débuté le 16 au 27 mai 2024 à Ouagadougou au Burkina Faso. Cette mission est réalisée par l'IFAD, impliquant que toutes modifications du Document Complet de Projet (DCP) doivent être portées à la connaissance des parties prenantes avant effet. La mission d'évaluation circulaire, qui a donc été conduite dans cette logique avec une attention particulière pour le cadre institutionnel et la gouvernance, est menée par Mme Aïda Héménière, Projets Climat (SDPC), accompagnée pour l'étude du Burkina Faso par M. Abdoul Aziz Ouédraogo, Responsable du Commissariat des développements de projets au sein du Bureau Régional du Projet LoCAL. La mission résidente (MR) de la BMG à été représentée par Mesdames ADJOVANDE-MARIE, Chef de l'Unité de Gestion des risques et Mme KOUYATE, Coordinatrice du projet Burkina Faso en tant que coordonnateur du travail. Le détail des personnalités et structures représentées la partie burkinabè est au besoin annexé à ce rapport.

## PROGRAMME RÉGIONAL DE FINANCEMENT DE L'ADAPTATION AU NIVEAU LOCAL

Le Programme Régional de Financement de l'Adaptation au Niveau Local (LoCAL) HRM est décliné autour de deux composantes, à savoir : [i] le renforcement des capacités de sensibilisation et de communication locales pour favoriser la prise en compte des risques climatiques dans les stratégies d'adaptation locales et ii) l'établissement de mécanismes de financement basés sur le pays pour soutenir l'investissement local. Les domaines d'intervention du programme sont : [i] la gestion des ressources naturelles localement menacées par la réchauffement climatique, les inondations et les sécheresses ; [ii] la transition énergétique vers une économie basée sur l'énergie renouvelable, les infrastructures résilientes au climat et les zones côtières. Les mesures clés du programme visent à : [i] renforcer les capacités institutionnelles, techniques et financières au niveau local ; (ii) réduire le coût des coûts supplémentaires des investissements résilients au climat ; (iii) la mise en œuvre d'initiatives pilotes pour l'évaluation de l'impact des interventions sur les écosystèmes, le secteur privé local et gérer des investissements alignés sur les priorités d'adaptation.

### Composante 1 : Renforcement des capacités de réponse et de sensibilisation au niveau local

#### Activités retenues
- **Activité 1.1 : Evaluation des stratégies prioritaires locales pour la sensibilisation et la renforcement des capacités**.
- **Activité 1.2 : Sensibilisation et formation des principales parties prenantes locales**.
- **Activité 1.3 : Mise en place d'une stratégie de sensibilisation et de communication locales**.

#### Résultats attendus
- Sécurisation accrue aux risques climatiques au niveau local.
- Amélioration de l'accès aux données nécessaires à la prise de décision locale par les parties prenantes.
- Renforcement des capacités des acteurs locaux pour une adaptation efficace menée localement.

### Composante 2 : Financement basé sur le pays

#### Activités retenues
- **Activité 2.1 : Mise en place de Systèmes d'Information Géographique (SIG) et de cartographie pour la prise en compte des risques climatiques**.
- **Activité 2.2 : Renforcement des capacités des gouvernements locaux cibles**.
- **Activité 2.3 : Évaluation des coûts supplémentaires des investissements résilients au climat pour la prise en compte participative et sensibilée au genre de l'adaptation**.

#### Résultats attendus
- Amélioration de la gestion des risques climatiques au niveau local.
- Renforcement des capacités des acteurs locaux pour une adaptation efficace menée localement.

### Composante 3 : Renforcement des capacités des gouvernements locaux

#### Activités retenues
- **Activité 3.1 : Évaluation des stratégies prioritaires locales pour la prise en compte des risques climatiques**.
- **Activité 3.2 : Renforcement des capacités des acteurs locaux pour évaluer et gérer les investissements en adaptation**.

#### Résultats attendus
- Amélioration de l'efficacité des stratégies d'adaptation au niveau local.

# Annexe 03 : Arrangement Institutionnel du Programme LoCAL au Burkina Faso

## Catégorisation du projet au niveau national

Sur le plan national, en fonction de leur rattachement et de leur mode de gestion, les projets et programmes de développement sont classés en quatre (4) catégories. Les catégories applicables au présent projet sont les suivantes :

- **Catégorie 1 : Projet exécuté directement par l'administration publique** ;
- **Catégorie 2 : Projet nécessitant pour sa mise en œuvre, la création d'une structure autonome d'exécution placée sous la tutelle de l'administration publique.**

En regard des critères de classification et dans un souci d'efficacité, le choix s'est porté sur la catégorie 2 : Projet nécessitant pour sa mise en œuvre la création d'une structure autonome d'exécution placée sous la tutelle de l'administration publique.

## Entité d'exécution et Unité de Gestion du Projet (UGP)

Au Burkina Faso, l'entité d'exécution (EE) du projet est le Fonds d'Intervention pour l'Environnement (FIE), un Fonds d'Etat sous la tutelle technique du Ministère de l'Environnement, de l'Eau et de l'Assainissement (MEEA). La principale mission du FIE est de contribuer à la réalisation des objectifs environnementaux du Burkina Faso, notamment la réduction de la dégradation de l'environnement et des pertes économiques associées, ainsi que la lutte contre les effets négatifs du changement climatique. Par ailleurs, le FIE est un instrument financier robuste capable de mobiliser des financements nationaux et internationaux pour soutenir l'atteinte des objectifs environnementaux.

Le FIE, potentiellement accrédité auprès du FVC et du Fonds d'adaptation (passage au stage 2) ou du processus d'accréditation du FVC, joue un rôle fiduciaire dans la mise en œuvre des Plans de développement intégré communautaire (PDIC) avec la Banque mondiale ainsi que le projet PADD/REDD+ financé par la Banque Africaine de Développement (BAD). Par exemple, dans le cadre de la mise en œuvre du projet PADD/REDD+, la mécanisme financier utilisé pour le déploiement des ressources s'est basé sur la performance du réseau des Caisse Populaires du Burkina Faso (RCPB) qui ont une expérience significative dans l'accompagnement collectif et qui couvre l'ensemble du territoire national. Cette collaboration s'est materialisée par la signature d'un accord entre le FIE et la RCPB.

Cette Unité de Gestion du Projet (UGP) sera mise en place au cours du mois de mai, les membres de l'UGP seront recrutés par appel à candidatures pour la coordination et le suivi de la mise en œuvre du projet. Elle est composée de :

- un (1) Coordinateur (féminin) ;
- un (1) Expert (en) suite évaluation ;
- un (1) Expert (en) et sauvegarde environnementale et sociale ;
- un (1) Comptable ;
- un (1) Analyste financier (féminin).

# Mission d'évaluation du/du Burkina Faso – Programme LoCAL, du 10 au 27 Mai 2021, Hôp Mémoire

## Secrétariat technique LOCAL

Dans le cadre du déploiement du Météorème LoCAL au Burkina Faso, il a été mis en place un Comité technique LoCAL par arrêté n°T368/MEAT/CAL du 14 juillet 2022 portant création, attributions et composition d'un Comité Technique du LoCAL de financement de l'adaptation au changement climatique et météorème local à LoCAL (Météorème LoCAL au Burkina Faso). La mission principale de ce Comité est de coordonner et de suivre la mise en œuvre du Météorème LoCAL au Burkina Faso. Placé sous la présidence du FIE, ce comité dispose d'un Secrétariat technique composé de membres désignés du FIE, de l'Autorité Nationale Désignée du Fonds vert pour le climat (ANDFPC), de la Direction Générale du Développement Territorial (DGDT) et de la Direction Générale des Collectivités Territoriales (DGT).

A ces structures, il conviendra d'ajouter la Direction Générale de la Coopération (DGCOOP) et l'Agence Nationale d'Aide au Développement des Collectivités Territoriales (ADACT). Dans le cadre du présent projet, le Comité technique LoCAL en collaboration avec l'UOP pour l'alignement des différents documents/livraisons avec le marché de la construction, a décidé de créer un CPT. Il collaborera avec l'UOP pour lever les goulots d'étranglement qui pourrait entraver la mise en œuvre du projet. Pour finir, le secrétariat technique contribuera à la consolidation des documents préparés par l'UOP avant transmission au Comité de Pilotage (COPIL) qui est composé de la majorité des membres du Comité Technique LoCAL.

### Comité de pilotage (COPIL)

Afin de faciliter la mise en œuvre du projet, un Comité de Pilotage (COPIL) sera mis en place conformément aux dispositions du décret n°2013-1389/RES/PAM/MEF du 31 décembre 2013 portant réglementation générale des projets et programmes de développement exécutés au Burkina Faso. Le COPIL sert d'organe d'orientation et de pilotage du projet. Le COPIL se réunira en session ordinaire deux (02) fois par an pour superviser la mise en œuvre du projet et renrer compte des progrès accomplis, il peut toutefois se réunir en session extraordinaire sur convocation du son Président.

Conformément à la réglementation en vigueur, le COPIL est composé de membres statutaires et de membres observateurs.

- The programme is consistent with the functions and objectives of the fund, in conformity with the national priorities, strategies and plans of Mali.

We also confirm that our no objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of the programme.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Secretariat's assessment of FP247

Proposal name: Local Climate Adaptive Living Facility Plus (LoCAL+)- West Africa (Burkina Faso, Ivory Coast, Mali and Niger)

Accredited entity: Banque Ouest Africaine de Développement (BOAD)

Countries: Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Mali, Niger

Project/programme size: Medium

### Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

#### Strengths
- The programme proposes innovative solutions to improve, at scale, the adaptive capacity of communities in the target countries through improved access to climate information and services.
- It improves the resilience of communities and other local actors at the centre of adaptation action by providing them with tools that contribute towards ensuring that adaptation actions and investments are located directly addressing the adaptations needs of vulnerable groups and their localities.

#### Points of caution
- As three of the countries are based with social and institutional fragility, there are additional risks for a timely and synchronized implementation of mitigation measures.
- A series of mitigation measures have been built into the project design to mitigate these risks. The phased disbursement per country and realization clauses and provision of a regional platform to share experiences and learn from each other.

The focus on promoting the participation of local actors, especially communities and local-private sector partnerships, small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs), and the deployment of innovative financial mechanisms to facilitate their involvement in the implementation of adaptation actions and investments is commendable. The countries are upholding the principles of locally led adaptation including stimulating local capabilities and institutional arrangements that are critical for achieving the devolution of decision-making to the lowest appropriate level, which is essential for reducing inequalities faced by marginalized groups in the society such as women, children, and youth, making predictable adaptation finance more easily accessible.

The programme's emphasis on strengthening governance systems at the national and local levels of implementation and ensuring their implementation through both national and regional institutions is commendable, because it


---
**Page 76**
---

### Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

#### Project background

1. The Local Climate Adaptive Living Facility (LoCAL+) – West Africa programme is an intervention aimed at promoting climate resilience among communities and local economies in Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Mali, Niger, Senegal, and Togo.
2. LoCAL+ will provide these countries with knowledge and know-how to identify and plan for response to climate change risks and impacts, and with improved access to adaptation finance for delivering climate-resilient investments in key areas including agriculture, water resources, health, energy efficiency, renewable energy, and infrastructure.
3. The objective of the programme is to enhance the institutional, technical and financial capacities of local governments and communities to plan and implement climate-resilient investments, thereby improving the improved capacities to stimulate the integration of climate resilience into standard development planning processes in these countries.
4. The West Africa region is home to a large number of fragile communities which are highly dependent on the local ecosystems for their survival, and are now facing agricultural losses resulting from increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events and environmental degradation induced by increasing temperatures and shifting rainfall patterns.
5. The extreme climate variability in this region has already had significant impacts that have resulted in challenges to livelihoods, food security and economic and governance stability.

#### Programme outcomes

1. The programme will overcome these challenges by helping to empower local governments and communities with the capacity to plan for and implement investments based on locally identified solutions and priorities, and by supporting locally-led investments in key areas including agriculture, water resources, health, energy efficiency, renewable energy, and infrastructure.
2. The programme is requesting EUR 43.8 million of GCF financing. The total cost of the programme, including or financing, is EUR 51.2 million, with co-financing primarily in the form of government contributions from Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Mali, Niger, Senegal Union, Council of Territorial Communities/Union Économique Monétaire Ouest Africaine, Conseil des Collectivités Territoriales (WAEMC) CTU/WEMCTA and the governments of the participating countries.

#### Environmental and social safeguards

1. In terms of environmental and social safeguards (E&S), this proposal has been categorized as a low-risk project (Low Risk – No Mitigation/Compensation).
2. The review by the Secretariat confirms the E&S risk category agreed with the AE.


---
**Page 77**
---

2.2 Component-by-component analysis

### Component 1: The awareness and response capacities of local governments, local actors and communities are strengthened and promote local adaptation responses (total cost: EUR 15.63 million; GCF cost: EUR 9.87 million)

- **Component 1** will provide dedicated technical assistance to strengthen the capacity of local governments and stakeholders to identify climate risks and plans for and implement mitigation and adaptation measures, including those that address climate change risks and impacts at the local level; (ii) improving the availability of and access for local stakeholders to finance for adaptation investments; and (iii) strengthening the capacities of local governments to plan, budget, implement and monitor for effective locally led adaptation. This component will also support the development of national-level adaptation investment proposals and to access blended financing instruments. This component clearly demonstrates how the programme can be scaled up in other countries and complementing the efforts of vulnerable local governments and communities in the target countries to identify, assess and develop plans for sustainable investment and financing of the additional climate change adaptation measures.

### Component 2: Country-based financing mechanisms to access and channel climate finance at the local level (total cost: EUR 34.92 million; GCF cost: EUR 31.85 million)

- **Component 2** will support the development of mechanisms for stimulating, enabling and promoting private sector investment in climate change adaptation measures in priority sectors of the participating countries. This component will facilitate the establishment and strengthening of public-private partnerships (PPPs) to mobilize private sector finance at the local level by supporting the institutionalization and scaling out of the LeCAL Performance-Based Climate Resilience Grant system in the four target countries, and by supporting the establishment of a national-level PPP framework for climate adaptation. This will enable private sector adaptation finance for revenue-generating investments at the local level. This component clearly demonstrates how the programme can be scaled up in other countries to catalyse and promote the numerous advantages and benefits that enabled direct access to financing for adaptation and locally driven action across vulnerable communities.

### Programme management and coordination (total cost: EUR 2.06 million; GCF cost: EUR 2.06 million)

- Project implementation and monitoring and evaluation costs have been adequately provided in the budget. The programme will be managed through a central unit within the Secretariat to ensure that project implementation and monitoring and evaluation processes will be coordinated.

### Gender and environment and social management framework aspects

- A cost environmental and social management framework (ESMF) was developed for the programme, which includes a gender action plan and an ESS safeguard plan. The ESS safeguards are put in place and observed throughout the programme's lifespan. A well-designed gender assessment will be conducted at the beginning of the programme to develop plans of actions that will be undertaken in the four programme countries to ensure that gender equality, equity and safeguards will be upheld throughout the lifespan of the programme.


---
**Page 78**
---

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

The West African region where the project sites are located, faces significant climate variability and change, affecting livelihoods, food security, and economic stability. Since the 1970s, extreme weather events have led to agricultural losses, food crises, water scarcity, flooding, and desertification. The region is projected to experience warming that exceeds the global average, with the Sahel region experiencing the most significant warming.

The programme seeks to support countries in addressing these climate challenges by quantifying adaptation needs and developing a portfolio of adaptation investments.

- **A1:** Most vulnerable people and communities (30%)
- **A2:** Health and well-being, food and water security (30%)
- **A3:** Infrastructure and built environment (20%)
- **A4:** Agriculture and land use (10%)

A consultative exercise in each country undertaken as part of the project development resulted in a list of eligible adaptation investments based on specific conditions and climate risks. The programme will support countries to identify, design, and implement projects that address socio-economic and environmental challenges and opportunities. Local governments and communities will be involved in the identification and implementation process.

The portfolio of adaptation investments will be selected through consultations and assessment of conditions to ensure effective and efficient GCF funding deployment. It will support local governments, actors, and communities in identifying and implementing adaptation measures at scale that are locally owned based on robust evidence of localized climate risks and impacts.

The programme is expected to directly benefit 604,982 beneficiaries and 3,499 indirect beneficiaries. It would effectively increase climate change resilience by up to 15% local governments in four countries by providing technical assistance (component 1), performance-based financing instruments (component 2 activity 2.1), and blended finance instruments (component 2 activity 2.2).

Source: Funding proposal annexal 13/24

The programme will specifically target adaptation investments in seven core areas for local climate resilience building in the four countries: climate change-adapted agriculture, forestry, agroforestry, water and health, energy efficiency, renewable energies, climate resilient infrastructure, and disaster risk management. The programme will also support the mobilization of public, locally led climate-resilient investments. The programme also aims to address adaptation finance constraints of local private sector actors through the blended finance facility.

# 3.2 Paradigm shift potential

## Scale: High

The programme demonstrates high paradigm shift potential in that it is the first time that a financial innovation has been piloted using the PFCG system to support both governments (public) and private sector adaptation investments in the region. This model will mobilize GCF’s financial products, local financial sector products, and private sector bankable projects, which are not currently available for adaptation finance.

The programme is envisaged to improve SMEs and local producer organizations’ access to finance through flexible eligibility criteria and innovative financing mechanisms.

The paradigm shift potential is well embedded across the two components of the programme and is well designed to be impactful over the long term. It particularly demonstrates:

- Transformational planning and programming: The programme aims to promote strategic, participatory, and inclusive planning and decision making, including new investment models that can inform the programming of Readiness, NAPs, NDCs, local strategic plans. The programme seeks to establish the LoCAL and blended finance mechanisms for adaptation investments in the region through national and international climate finance through individual Financial Agreements with in-country financial institutions and/or a dedicated Facility managed by the GCF Implementing Partner, providing expertise and quality assurance for the design, implementation, and monitoring of projects, thereby coordinating LoCAL and attracting domestic and international climate finance.

- Catalyzing climate innovation: When the proposal was initially submitted to the secretariat, it was a traditional LoCAL architecture with the business-as-usual interventions that did not consider the specific needs of vulnerable communities in the region. Access to robust climate risk information and decision support tools will help to better drive private sector uptake of local climate action was not adequately articulated.

The secretariat project team then worked with BOAD to explore a number of paradigm-shifting ideas from the GCF portfolio for consideration, which include an innovative financing


---
**Page 80**
---

an area-wide implementation arrangement that builds on baseline interventions in the region that have demonstrated some level of good success and room for improvement. ROAD took on the challenge and commissioned a study to mobilize best practices and lessons in the region to re-define how a successful LoCAL and other decentralized local climate action intervention arrangements can be scaled up across the region. The study also considered the micro-finance institutions within the intervention areas for local climate action and enabling coordination of their activities with the national financial sector, as well as the GCF envisaged to ensure coherence, complementarity and learning at all levels. This has the potential to contribute to regional cooperation and trade among the participating communities and beyond.

The programme proposes a climate risk-informed ACF/MAE framework for evaluating adaptation needs and impacts of climate change on local level governments to help track progress and improve in increasing community resilience and local economic development. The framework is designed with a focus on the strategic alignment of project implementation in meeting GCF and programme outcomes. Additionally, it provides a mechanism for assessing the potential impact and potential of initiatives for effective local adaptation finance and action. It also ensures the rights of indigenous communities in each of the four countries are inherently addressed in the design and implementation.

The programme also adopts a systems approach to comprehensively integrate feedback loops into project design and implementation processes through participatory risk valuation. As a result, it adequately captures dynamic policy and decision-making processes across a range of spatial-temporal scales.

The programme supports the adoption of digital transformation at the local level (e.g., e-infrastructure, IoT, big data analytics, and connectivity) to help reduce costs and enhance resilience. The use of digital technologies in climate risk management can enhance financial transactions and utility of critical resources. Widely adopting suitable technologies can enhance locally-led anticipatory approaches and help reduce loss and damage, while promoting the development of a resilient local economy. It also supports collaboration with the national meteorological and hydrological services, regional climate centers (ACMAD and AGRHYMET), and other relevant stakeholders to support the generation of climate data and innovation. Since the programme targets intervention at the local communities, it should ensure that project design and implementation processes are informed by the temporal and spatial details of climate risks are made widely available. It can learn from the county level climate services in the region through the World Bank’s Level seven information presented in European National Framework for Climate Services.

Mobilization of finance on scale: It aims to enhance access to domestic and international climate funds, including GCF, by strengthening local financial institutions and facilities, and make disaster risk finance more accessible to local governments, communities, and financial institutions operating micro-finance institutions.

Enabling coordination among financial institutions, including micro-finance institutions in intervention areas, coordinated by an effective regional arrangement, promotes coherence, complementarity, and learning at all levels. It also fosters innovation and adaptation and innovation. It promotes a proven incentive-based mechanism to create an enabling environment for engaging local governments and actors into the expertise and resources to promote integration of adaptation needs and actions into development plans, budgets and processes.

This is the first time such an approach is being applied in the communities to enable climate finance to reach the most vulnerable communities to demonstrate that security of

# Sustainable development potential

The programme contributes directly towards efforts to achieve the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), especially SDG1 (No poverty); SDG5 (Gender equality); SDG6 (Decent work and economic growth); SDG7 (Clean energy access and consumption); and SDG 13 (Climate action).

The programme makes a significant align/contributions to the GCF targeted results for 2024-2027 (DSP 2), specifically:

* **Target 1 (Developing countries directly supported to advance NDC/NAP/ITS implementation):** The four developing countries supported to implement their NDC/NAP/ITS.
* **Target 2 (Direct Access Entities):** Build a Direct Access Entity.
* **Target 3 (Climate Information and Early Warning Systems):** Project areas in the four countries will be covered by climate information and early warning systems.
* **Target 4 (Food):** promote investment in transformative agricultural practices
* **Target 5 (Ecosystems):** Promote investment in ecosystem-based adaptation measures
* **Target 6 (Infrastructure):** Social and community infrastructure made resilient through investments in the four countries.
* **Target 7 (Clean Energy) Renewable energy sources to drive the interventions
* **Target 9 (Adaptation and Locally-led Action):** The proposal is an adaptation and locally-led action project.
* **Target 10 (Local private sector early-stage ventures and MSME innovation):** The project will support the development of a blended Finance Facility that will be deployed to provide financial services for local private sector early-stage ventures and MSMEs. This facility shall have the capacity of MSMEs to identify and develop home-grown innovative climate solutions.
* **Target 11 (National and regional financial institutions):** Implementation would be through financial institutions at the global, regional, national and subnational levels including development banks.

To comprehensively assess the potential social, economic and environmental co-benefits, the programme uses a systems dynamic and sustainable asset valuation approach to explore the potential for improved livelihoods in the intervention areas and decision-making processes that could result in sustained livelihoods in the intervention areas.


---
**Page 82**
---

It demonstrates scenarios that could reduce climate-related costs and generate non-benefits, supporting livelihoods and climate-resilient communities. It also shows how the co-benefits complement priority multi-sectoral/integrated interventions, such as water retention, forest restoration, solar water pumps, sustainable agriculture practices, and ecosystem-quality.

## Needs of the recipient

### Section: High

1. The programme targets one of the world’s most vulnerable regions, where water, food and livelihood insecurity conditions arise from the various hazards posed by extreme climate variability, which are compounded by the high political fragility in the target countries.
2. The proposed facilities will help communities to continue their livelihoods while also supporting them in diversifying their current livelihood strategies and yet they lack the financial resources and access to credit facilities to support their effort to secure their livelihoods and natural capital from the impacts of climate change, which are expected to increase in intensity and frequency.
3. Without access to concessional finance and insurance mechanisms, communities will be unable to adapt and unable to secure concessional finance internationally. Without access to concessional finance and insurance mechanisms, communities will not have the means to provide vulnerable communities in the target countries with the safety nets they need.

### Section: High

#### Country ownership

4. Country ownership is well embedded in the programme, which is fully aligned with the climate change and broader development aspirations, strategies and policies of all four target countries. The programmes aim at building local government capacity at both regional and national levels to identify actors in the target countries to identify their risks and impacts that they face and to identify their own solutions and means to implement them.
5. The programme has been developed with strong participation and consultation from key national, regional and local institutions in both the public and private sectors and will help to build capacity at all levels of government, including at the sub-national level, building access for communities to resilient infrastructure and health, water and food systems.

#### Section: High

6. The AIF is the West African Development Bank/Banque Ouest Africaine de Développement (BOAD), which has a long-standing presence in the target countries, and a wealth


---
**Page 84**
---

## Efficiency and effectiveness

### Scale: Medium to high

The programme will directly support poor and vulnerable communities in the participating countries to strengthen their ability to secure, diversify and transform their own adaptive capacity and livelihood systems.

It will target 52,400 direct beneficiaries and 3.49 million indirect beneficiaries over a period of five years (2018–2023).

The programme is designed to provide communities with access to finance for communities and MSEMs to participate meaningfully in underlying resilience-building activities.

The funding amount is well justified to provide the support needed to unlock the transformation of livelihoods and local health, water and food systems and to provide innovative solutions that can be scaled up across West Africa and beyond into participating countries and in the West African region in general. The programme is fostering an enabling environment for climate resilience by supporting the development of Performance-Based Climate Resilience Grant mechanisms and blended financing facilities, through which communities will have access to finance for their activities.

The programme also has an innovative exit strategy that will enable scaling up its approach to finance across West Africa and will build access and partnership with local governments and other stakeholders in order to address climate change challenges with innovative and locally appropriate and developed solutions.


## Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

The LaCALA facility aims to support adaptation investments across seven key intervention areas – identified as priorities for local communities in the four West African countries.

The facility is categorised having a low level of intermediation (13), given that the proposed portfolio of investments includes financial exposure to activities that predominantly have no environmental or social risks and/or impacts. The Secretariat confirms this categorisation, which is within the AIF’s accreditation limit. Only Category 2 projects with significant environmental or social risks and/or impacts will be excluded.

The LaCALA facility has developed an environmental and social management framework (ESMF) to ensure that all investments are consistent with the development of appropriate environmental and social action plans on activities that may have minimal environmental and social risks and/or impacts.


---
**Page 85**
---

# Gender policy

## 4.2 Gender policy

The AI has provided a gender analysis and action plan and therefore complies with the gender policy of the fund.

The gender analysis was conducted through desk reviews as well as consultations with communities and consultations at community levels in the three out of the four countries sought the views of women and women's groups. The desk review ensured to capture and incorporate lessons from a similar and previously conducted project, on the positive practices


---
**Page 86**
---

that promoted women’s empowerment and enhanced women’s participation. All four countries, though committed to the empowerment of women and have policies and strategies in place, have gaps in implementing the policies and strategies to lift women and achieve the desired equality between women and men. Many interventions and investments exist in these countries that address gender issues, but there are still significant challenges. The following table (in the three countries) useful improvements to women and their decision-making position in risk management and climate change adaptation processes. These include the introduction of gender-sensitive trainings which has helped elected officials and local authorities better serve vulnerable groups such as women and children. Other investments have focused on training for women’s groups which helped the women start small businesses, and other initiatives that address the needs of women and youth. These are some of the encouraging changes that has informed the design of the gender action plan for the project.

The gender action plan is a comprehensive document with a focus also on gender-based differences in capacity and access needs of climate data and information, climate risk planning and adaptation processes. The gender action plan includes activities that will address several challenges to women particularly in accessing resources and participating in planning and decision-making processes. The lack of engagement of women in the planning process results from limited awareness about their vulnerability to climate change impacts and for leaving them behind in accessing needed resources and information. This is further compounded by the lack of participation and the limited space for their engagement in planning processes resulting in limited number of women in leadership roles as well as in the presence and voice of women in other decision-making positions. The gender action plan also addresses the burden of multiple responsibilities (domestic and productive roles) limited access to land, credit, and other resources that are critical for women’s economic empowerment and investment and other economic activities. Additionally, the lack of enabling environment and institutions as well as lack of understanding and awareness of the depth of needs of women and other gender-based differences in capacity and access needs of climate data and information. There are gaps in leadership roles for women as well as gaps in the presence and voice of women in other decision-making positions. The gender action plan aims to address these presented gaps in terms of capacity, awareness, creating access to resources to women, engaging women in various activities and decision making processes, and increasing their representation in groups. The gender action plan defines activities, indicators, and targets for women’s participation in planning and decision-making processes. The gender action plan also calls on local institutions will be mobilized to support the implementation of the gender action plan along with budgets to implement the gender action plan.

### 4.3 Risks

#### 4.3.1 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

The proposed adaptation programme for the four countries in West Africa – Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Mali and Niger – aims to support investment in areas such as agriculture, forestry, water resources management and disaster risk reduction. The overall programme plan is work with national institutions and local government in the four countries to increase their capacity for climate change adaptation through a combination of technical assistance and projects.

GCF has been requested to provide EUR 48.38 million. Together with grant and in-kind contribution of EUR 0.44 million from the WAMU CTC/UEMOA CCT and agencies from the four countries, the total amount for the overall programme plan is EUR 52.82 million. The borrowing ratio is 1:0.018 and accounts for 15 per cent of total financing.


---
**Page 87**
---

### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)

#### a1)
The Bank of Africa, a regional financial institution in West Africa mandated to promote development and economic integration in the subregion. BOAD mobilizes a combination of local resources from member States/territories and foreign capital to invest in climate mitigation and adaptation projects. As part of its policy mandate for sustainable economic growth and poverty reduction, BOAD has a history of working with GCF after it was first accredited in August 2017 and is currently the only financial institution in West Africa that has received funding proposals with BOAD for cross-cutting and mitigation projects in the subregion.

#### a2)
BOAD will work with four national institutions as EEs, the National Environment Fund for the Environment (FNE), the National Agency for the Promotion of Investment and Local Development (Gite D'Investissement), the National Agency for Local Governments’ Investment (Mali) and the National Agency for the Promotion of Investment and Local Development (Guinea). These entities have received funding from BOAD in the past, either directly or through channeled funding for their respective countries. The United Nations Capital Development Fund (UNCDF) will serve as a technical advisor to BOAD and provide support in developing project proposals and recommendations to each national technical committee. Established in 1966, UNCDF has experience working in 82 developing countries overseeing finance work with public and private resources.

### 4.3.3 Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

#### a)
The proposed programme plans to strengthen local government adaptation responses and implement country-based mechanisms to channel climate finance at the local level. Several countries have limited public resources, and their ability to absorb new funding for climate action will be partly mitigated by the AE conducting comprehensive political analysis during project preparation and implementation phases. The AE will also conduct a risk assessment that includes engagement with stakeholders and closely monitoring national security situations. To help create an enabling environment, the AE will also engage in proactive policy dialogue with host governments to ensure alignment of their climate change policies with national development risk. Governance risk will be managed by regular financial audits and annual performance assessments.

### 4.3.4 Programme viability and concessionality

#### a)
The proposed programme has outlined two activities under its funding component, namely: the performance-based climate resilience grant and blended finance facilities to catalyze private sector investment in climate adaptation projects. The performance-based climate resilience grant is based on the experience of UNCDF as the implementing partner that designed the Local Climate Adaptive Living Facility (LoCAL) pilot project launched at 14 countries. The programme will rely on the AE’s ability to identify and channel focused blended financing for each country. The proposed GCF grant will represent 85 per cent of total programme size in a wholly grant-funded programme. The proposed blended finance facility is expected to leverage private sector investment (countries with low credit ratings [in the CCC range]). These countries have limited public resources and their ability to absorb new funding for climate action.

#### 4.4 Fiduciary

The West African Development Bank/Banque Ouest Africaine du Développement will be the accredited entity (AE) responsible for the implementation of the programme in close collaboration with UNCDF and other international financial institutions. The AE will also collaborate with national institutions as highlighted below. UNCDF will play the role of implementing partner, providing key support and expertise and ensuring the effective implementation of the LGAL standard – in combination with the West African Development Bank.

As the AE of the programme, BRAD will sign a funded activity agreement (FAA) with GCF, defining the terms and conditions for the use of GCF funds. The FAA will also define roles for the implementation of specific activities of the programme in accordance with the FAA. On-granting agreements will be signed between the AE and the national institutions (EEs) that will manage the specific activities. The EEs will be responsible for the execution of the feasibility studies (the Intervention Fund for the Environment in Burkina Faso; the Directorate General of Water Resources and Irrigation in Niger; the National Agency for the Promotion of Local Governments’ Investment in Mali; and the National Agency for the Financing of Local Governments in Niger).

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP247

## Proposal name:
Local Climate Adaptive Living Facility Plus (LoCAL+) - West Africa (Burkina Faso, Ivory Coast, Mali and Niger)

## Accredited entity:
Banque Ouest Africaine de Développement (BOAD)

## Countries:
Burkina Faso, Ivory Coast, Mali, Niger

## Project/programme size:
Medium

### Assessment of the Independent Technical Advisory Panel
#### 1. Overview
The Local Climate Adaptive Living Facility Plus (LoCAL+) regional programme covers four countries in West Africa, namely Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Mali and Niger. The submission is for phase two of LoCAL+, which builds on the success of the first public sector adaptation programme in environmental and social safeguards risk category C. The Banque Ouest Africaine de Développement (BOAD) is the accredited entity (AE).

The LoCAL+ programme aims to increase countries' resilience to climate change, with limited readiness to address these challenges. Targeted local adaptation solutions are needed at subnational levels in order to reduce vulnerability and enhance adaptive capacity.

However, capacity gaps and the availability of climate finance for adaptation action at subnational levels remain constraints, and adaptation investments are considered too risky by the private sector without additional financial incentives.

LoCAL+ proposes to promote climate-resilient communities and economies in the four countries through a multi-faceted approach that includes strengthening local governance and budgeting systems, raising awareness of climate change impacts, and increasing the availability of funding for local governments and the private sector for climate adaptation action.

The LoCAL+ programme is based on the Local Climate Adaptation Facility (LoCAF) which was designed by the United Nations Capital Development Fund (UNCDF), that direct performance-based financing to subnational governments in developing countries to cover the additional costs of climate-resilient investments. UNCDF has supported 18 countries through its LoCAF programmes and the results are promising.

The LoCAL+ programme is divided into three phases. So far, phase one has been piloted in a few local governments in Burkina Faso, Mali, and Niger, with Côte d'Ivoire now ready to come on board. LoCAL+ will support phase two to extend the programme to its full scale in all four countries. The aim is to increase the availability of climate adaptation funding into local government systems so that LoCAL+ can be scaled up in phase three.

The LoCAL+ programme also introduces a new dedicated blended finance facility in each country through which local private sector actors can access repayable grants for income-generating activities.

The funding proposal was submitted to the independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) on 9 August 2024. This assessment is based on the ITAP's deliberations and answers further discussed in an online meeting between the ITAP and the accredited entity on 2 September 2024.

7. Each country has developed a predetermined menu of eligible investments that will guide which activities are funded through the FBCRG and the blended finance facilities. The menu of eligible investments includes infrastructure, transport, urban development, energy, agroforestry, water and health, energy efficiency and renewable energies, climate-resilient infrastructure, and disaster risk management.

8. The executing entities (EE) for the programme comprise:

(a) The West African Economic and Monetary Union Council of Local Communities (UEMOA CCL), Burkina Faso;

(b) The Intervention Fund for the Environment (FIE, Burkina Faso);

(c) The General Directorate of Decentralization and Local Development (DGDDL, Côte d'Ivoire);

(d) Agence Nationale d'Investissement des Collectivités Territoriales [Local Governments National Investment Agency] (ANICT, Mali); and

(e) the National Agency for the Financing of Local Governments (ANFICF, Niger).

Key implementing partners include the Confederation of Financial Institutions of West Africa (CFIWA), which will provide technical assistance to UEMOA CCL in administering the blended finance facilities. CIF will utilize its network of member financial institutions to mobilize and disburse funds for eligible investments through credit or equity financial products for local small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs). The UNCDF will provide a range of support services across all programme components and will support dialogue between local governments.

10. The governance and management structures include the establishment of a regional project steering committee, country-level steering committees, and national focal points.

11. The total budget of EUR 52.46 million comprises a request for CCF grant funds of EUR 48.38 million, with contributions of EUR 8.43 million comprising grants of EUR 7.18 million from UEMOA CCL and in-kind contributions totalling approximately EUR 1.26 million from the four countries involved.

12. The AEE, BOAD, was accredited to CIF in 2016 and is a recipient of four CIF programmes worth a total USD 3.5 million, including in Niger (FF17F, 2021), Senegal (FF13H, 2020), Mali (FF16Z, 2019) and multiple countries in West Africa (FF18K, 2019).

### 1.2 Impact potential

The funding mechanism sets out that all five participating West African countries experience extreme hazards and are highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. Burkina Faso, a landlocked country located within the Sahel zone, is exposed to a highly variable climate characterized by low rainfall variability and high inter-annual precipitation extremes. Gite d'Inferre faces floods, storms, landslides, droughts, heatwaves, and bushfires, and its coastal areas are vulnerable to sea-level rise and storm surges. The country experienced severe droughts during the twentieth century and climate change in the country has resulted in a drier climate overall. Niger faces increasing spatial and temporal variability of rainfall, a trend towards rising


---
**Page 93**
---

# GCF R4/92 Add.099

Page 131

## Rainfall patterns have changed, with more extreme rainfall events projected in the future across all four countries. The region has also seen significant temperature increases over the past century, with an average increase of about 1°C by the end of this century.

Detailed annexes to the funding proposal include the identification and mapping of climate hazards and vulnerabilities for each country. These are supported by a vulnerability assessment on analysis of climate threats and their impacts for vulnerable sectors in Côte d’Ivoire, and a local climate change risk assessment for Burkina Faso. The GCF project design document (PDD) and RCP 8.5, county-level report predicts increases in temperatures and in the numbers of hot days, and thus heat stress.

The populations of these four countries are particularly vulnerable due to their reliance on agriculture and natural resources, and the impacts of climate change on food security and infrastructure will have significant adverse effects for the most vulnerable groups, and disproportionately affected.

## LoCALa will provide support through two components:

### Component 1: Strengthening local capacities

- **Output 1**: Capacities of national governments to strengthen local level adaptation are strengthened.
- **Output 2**: Capacities of local governments to plan, budget, implement and monitor for effective locally led adaptation actions are strengthened.

### Component 2: Country-based financing mechanisms to access and channel climate finance towards the most vulnerable sectors

- **Output 1**: Country-based financing mechanisms are established to channel climate finance towards the most vulnerable sectors.
- **Output 2**: Blended finance facilities are established, and revenue-generating adaptation investments are made in the sense of eligible investments in each country.

#### Note: Funding proposal as of 15/1

The funding proposal confirms that all public and private investments under the PBCRG mechanism will be subject to a pre-approval process. This ensures the pre-determination of eligible investments (Annexes ZA2, ZB2, ZC2 and ZD2) for which public and private eligible investments are categorized within seven key sectors and follow specific eligibility criteria that prioritize adaptation over mitigation. The list of potential projects and activities was identified in consultation with national and subnational stakeholders. They include activities that could be implemented by the government or by the private sector, depending on locally determined contributions (NDCs).

While the PBCRG component has been tested under phase 1 in three of the four participating countries, the blended finance facilities will be newly introduced to all four countries. However, in the view of the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP), while the types of investments that are being proposed for adaptation and mitigation are clear, the precise adaptation impacts that are being projected, the precise composition of the portfolio to be funded under each country’s NDCs, as well as the specific contributions from public and private sectors are yet to be selected and their capacities to select investments based on the menu are as yet unknown. These factors present some challenges in assessing the final impact potential.

Based on the above, the TAP assesses the input impact potential at medium.

### 1.3 Paradigm shift potential

The funding proposal clearly identifies how the programme proposes to tackle the main constraints that limit private sector participation in adaptation, which include lack of technical capacity and access to information for climate-risk-informed planning, chronic underfunding of adaptation investments by the public and private sectors, and limited financial capacities in meeting the adaptation needs at local level and contributing to resilience. The programme also recognizes constraints that limit private sector participation in adaptation, noting that there is a lack of understanding on how to integrate adaptation into business models and limited financial literacy. There is also a widespread lack of understanding of how to integrate


---
**Page 95**
---

climate adaptation into their business models. Additionally, local microfinance institutions often lack the knowledge and expertise to attract climate finance.

23. The theory of change is logical, and the seven outputs to be delivered under two components (see table 1 above) cover the spectrum of support towards strengthening climate adaptation capacity at national level. By addressing these seven outputs in a coherent way, these efforts could help in fostering an enabling environment for climate adaptation action and bring about a transformational change in the institutional arrangements that are conducive to climate adaptation actions in a way that benefits local communities.

24. Component 1 is comprehensively detailed in the proposed work seeks to strengthen local capacity to make investments in climate resilience. The seven outputs under this component (outputs 1–3), awareness-raising and capacity-building will help local governments to integrate adaptation into local planning. The development of a national strategy for climate change adaptation at the national level in four of the countries is expected to deepen stakeholder understanding and enable countrywide access to climate information, which can be used to identify risks, vulnerabilities and opportunities and vulnerabilities when making planning and budgeting decisions. Output 4 will support local action to make investments in sustainable development investments through sensitization and awareness-raising activities.

25. Component 2 introduces two complementary mechanisms that envisage a paradigm shift towards financing climate adaptation at the national level (outputs 4–7).

(a) Outputs 5 and 6 will enable PBRCG funding for local governments through existing intergovernmental fund mechanisms. This funding is intended to institutionalize and mainstream climate change adaptation into local planning, budgeting and decision-making processes. These PBRCG funds will cover the additional costs of making investments climate-resilient and also provide a financial incentive for local governments to make such investments.

(b) Output 7 will establish financial facilities for local private sector actors, such as SMEs and producers’ organizations, to enable their participation in local adaptation activities. The facility will be structured as an insurance pool that provides a risk-sharing model for private sector projects on a repayable basis (in local currency) to ensure sustainability of the investments made by the private sector (and can also attract additional financial/donors) leveraging by leveraging the results of financed projects.

26. The potential for knowledge-sharing and learning is high within the regional PIMI and the regional climate change adaptation fund, which will be established under this project. The countries will also be able to join the global board of UNCCD’s LGaCL, with access to its wider network of partners and expertise.

27. The LGaCL mechanism is being undertaken incrementally in phases with a view to becoming the national system for financing climate change adaptation at the local level. Phase 1 (piloting) will be completed by June 2014. Phase 2 (roll-out) will introduce the PBRCGs over one or two fiscal cycles. Phase 2 (persons cover five to ten subnational governments) aims to establish a replicable model for financing climate adaptation at the local level, and to provide a realistic viable full national roll-out of the approach. Phase 3 (scaling-up) aims to roll out the PBRCG mechanism across the country. It also aims to leverage domestic and international finance for climate change adaptation in the country.

The four participating countries are at different phases of implementing LGaCL (see table 2). Côte d’Ivoire is currently preparing to enter phase 1, while Niger is preparing to enter phase 2, and Nigeria has 15 local governments already benefiting from PBRCG. Côte d’Ivoire is gearing up to move to phase 3. Côte d’Ivoire is preparing to enter the pilot phase 1.

29\. The selection of 85 local governments is yet to be confirmed and will be completed during phase one of the programme. These are expected to represent a cross-section of aspiring local governments including the country context, level of buy-in, availability of funds and levels of institutionalization. LoCAL technical committees which function as oversight supervisory bodies will be established.

30\. While the programme has the potential to bring about paradigm shifts in each of the countries involved, it is not clear how this will be achieved or what the expected impacts on countries according to levels of institutional buy-in, local capacities, access to reliable climate information, availability of funding, interest of financial institutions and SMEs, the evolving accountability mechanisms and other factors.

31\. The potential for uptake and scaling will largely depend on the capacity of local governments to absorb new knowledge and skills, as well as the extent that these are scalable and replicable. The number of local governments covered by phase 2 represents a small proportion of the overall population. A risk is that local governments that come on board midway through the programme may not be able to complete their adaptation plans within the remaining time frame of the programme.

32\. The funding proposal lacks clarity on what mechanisms will be put in place to ensure that local governments use USA I’s systems as they are set up, to draw on climate risk information to plan local investments. It is also not clear which agency will be responsible for maintaining and updating these systems. Furthermore, the funding proposal does not provide details of how climate risks will be factored into the proposed investment plans. The TAP has recommended that a dedicated technical assistance fund will be available to private sector stakeholders. It is also not clear how climate finance assessments will be applied in the context of the programme and whether this will be used as an additional adaptation investment measure. In the view of the TAP, this is a critical gap that carries the risk of investments being chosen without taking into account the highest adaptation priorities.

33\. As the blended financing mechanisms are still under development, it will also be important for a high success rate of returnable grants to be maintained so that the revolving facility can continue to provide funding. The ability of local governments to absorb and scale will also depend on the ability of the programme to sustain engagement with local private sector stakeholders as they invest in adaptation, and on how successfully the programme is able to establish a private sector investment pipeline for further investments into climate change adaptation.

34\. The sociopolitical context in these countries is a major factor in determining the extent to which the programme’s impacts may reasonably achieve a paradigm shift. Three of the four participating countries are affected by conflict, and there are significant risks associated with regional conflict, instability and security threats. Conflicts between ethnic groups are often driven by competition for resources and land.

35\. These institutions and risks could have profound implications for the LoCAL+ programme. The funding proposal set out its approach to a conflict resolution mechanism that could come into play.
