### Sustainable development potential

The programme is aligned with 12 of (17 of) the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), of which 6 are directly relevant to climate change adaptation. Other SDGs include: SDG 1 (No Poverty), SDG 2 (Zero Hunger), SDG 3 (Good Health and Well-being), SDG 5 (Gender Equality), SDG 8 (Decent Work and Economic Growth), SDG 9 (Industry, Innovation and Infrastructure), SDG 10 (Reduced Inequalities), SDG 11 (Sustainable Cities and Communities), SDG 15 (Life on Land) and SDG 17 (Partnerships for the Goals).

The programme is co-developed with a gender action plan (annex A6.2), an indigenous people’s plan (annex B6), a conflict sensitivity analysis (annex G6.a), a gender and social inclusion action plan (annex H6.a) and a gender assessment (Annex I6.y).

The programme co-developed with a gender action plan, conflict sensitivity analysis, and awareness-raising will help to sensitise communities and local stakeholders to the impacts of climate change. The programme also aims at promoting the development of tools that can support environmentally friendly practices, such as land restoration and integrated water resources management. The programme has been subjected to an environmental and social screening process (category B) in accordance with the UNDP’s Environmental and Social Management Framework. All investments will undergo environmental and social safeguards screening (category C) before final funding decisions are made.

### Social co-benefits

Social co-benefits are likely from reduced vulnerability of poor communities affected by climate change. Inclusive and participatory stakeholder engagement in planning and implementation is a key element to ensure that the programme benefits all vulnerable groups, including women, youth and minorities, are represented. Annex 6.b also outlines how these principles will be applied during the design phase of the programme, which is fundamentally inclusive in accordance with the customs, norms and values of the local communities. The management plan for the programme includes a gender action plan, adaptation and adaptation planning processes, and in the identification and design of subprojects.

### Gender co-benefits

Gender co-benefits could be realised through recognising the vulnerabilities faced by women and girls as well as men and boys to climate change impacts and ensuring their active participation in decision-making and implementation activities. A positive feature of the programme is that it will address gender equality across all dimensions, including social dimensions. The gender and social inclusion action plan (annex 8.a) outlines actions to advance gender equality through a participatory approach, which will be applied directly and indirectly. The programme also proposes to capture gender-disaggregated data wherever possible and will support local authorities in assessing the extent to which public adaptation investments are gender-sensitive. This is done by using gender indicators such as those used in the Women Empowerment Index, developed by UNCDF.

### Financial co-benefits

Financial co-benefits include increasing access to finance for communities and local SMEs and improving the enabling environment for local businesses to invest in adaptation actions. An assumption that is the blended finance


---
**Page 98**
---

facilities to support income-generating projects that reflect the adaptation priorities identified in local planning processes and are highlighted in the menu of investments. In the long run, capacity-building of stakeholders and the blended finance facilities could encourage financial institutions to adopt innovative financing mechanisms for climate-resilient infrastructure and climate-smart practices in their businesses while contributing to economic growth. Activities funded through this proposal include: (a) improving access to clean energy; (b) developing rural road and transport infrastructure; could help communities to apply more sustainable and productive land management practices, which would increase the resilience of agricultural systems less vulnerable to the effects of climate change. Similarly, coastal protection investments could help to reduce losses and assets to erosion and infrastructure.

The sustainable development potential of the project is assessed as high.


### 1.5 Needs of the recipient
Scale: High

All four participating countries in West Africa are highly vulnerable to climate change and climate-related disasters. In addition to the challenges posed by rising temperatures and shifting rainfall patterns are also affecting livelihoods, food security, and economic governance stability. Annex B8 highlights the vulnerability of women to the impacts of climate change and its effects on their access to resources, which can exacerbate social and political status. It notes gender disparities and inequalities in access to resources that disproportionately affect women.

Three of the four countries (Burkina Faso, Mali, and Niger) are Least Developed Countries, and face socioeconomic vulnerability, have unstable macroeconomic contexts, and are heavily dependent on agriculture for their livelihoods. In addition to these vulnerabilities, it faces several socio-economic disparities and poverty challenges. The populations at all four countries are highly vulnerable to climate change and its effects on the economy of fishing, which comprise significant proportions of the countries' gross domestic product (GDP). As of 2017, 58 per cent of GDP in Burkina Faso is from agriculture; 39 per cent of GDP in Côte d'Ivoire; 37 per cent of GDP in Mali and 38 per cent of GDP in Niger.

The funding proposal references the significant funding gap for adaptation highlighted in each country. The total estimated funding gap is USD 12 billion for the four countries, USD 0 million in Mali and USD 2.7 billion in Niger.


### 1.6 Country ownership
Scale: Medium

The national designated authorities of all four participating countries have provided no objection letters (annexes A-1 to H) and will contribute to kind-of co-financing.


---
**Page 99**
---

### At the national level, the proposed programme aligns with the respective countries' national strategies and action plans on climate change.

Each of the four countries has submitted its NDC to the UNFCCC Secretariat in accordance with the Paris Agreement (revised 2021). All four countries have submitted three national communications on climate change to the UNFCCC Secretariat, as required by the Paris Agreement.

The proposal is also aligned with each country's national policies for sustainable development and for decentralization. It is tailored to the political and institutional frameworks of each country.

### Annex 7 summarizes the overall approach to stakeholder consultations and the stakeholder engagement plan. The document does not seem to have been updated since it was produced in February 2020, although some of its content may be outdated. The multi-level consultative process to finalize programme design do not contain much information on the stakeholder groups that were consulted or how they were engaged.

However, annexes 68 outlines recent consultations with women during field missions in 2023 to gather feedback and lessons learned from the implementation of the UNICEF Local Action Project in the four targeted countries, namely Burkina Faso, Mali, and Niger. It also confirms that women will continue to be key stakeholders for engagement and consultation throughout programme implementation.

### Scoping visits for the design of the UNICEF Local project in Mali and Niger date back to July 2017. The scoping missions were conducted by a team from UNICEF headquarters, and entry points were identified. In these two countries, consultations continued throughout the implementation phase of the programme. These included multi-stakeholder discussions with central and local governments, Local technical committees, and the activities of development partners.

In Côte d'Ivoire, a scoping mission was carried out in May 2019 and a design process began in June 2019. The scoping mission for Benin took place between September 2019 and the design was finalized in February 2020.

### Further consultations with key stakeholders were initiated through a seminar in Ouagadougou, Burkina Faso, on 5-6 November 2019 to validate and finalize the content and modalities of the funding proposal. However, there is no detail available on what issues were discussed or how they were addressed.

### National-level consultations were carried out in all four countries, but they appear mainly focused on validation of national-level instruments and their implementation forms. These took place in Bamako, Mali (13–14 May 2024), Ouagadougou (16–17 May 2024), Niamey, Niger (20–21 May 2024) and Abidjan, Côte d'Ivoire (28 June 2024). The consultations in Bamako and Ouagadougou seem to have mainly involved national-level representatives; the extent to which they included women's organizations is unclear.

The annexes make reference to the LoCAL mechanisms with local governments. It is less clear how these stakeholder discussions focused specifically on the Menedélo instruments, which, in the view of the TAP, is a gap. The stakeholder engagement plan does not clearly outline how it will ensure that women's organizations are adequately consulted at all stages of implementation, or how the programme will ensure that the voices of communities are adequately reflected in programme implementation.

1.7 Efficiency and effectiveness

Scale: Medium

The co-financing ratio is low at 0.18%. The programme will receive co-financing from GEF/CCF (EUR 7,164,000) for activities under outputs 4 and 7, as well as in-kind financing from component 2.

A majority of GCF resources are allocated to component 2 (72 per cent), with the balance allocated to component 1 (23 per cent) and project management costs (5 per cent). Within component 2, the allocation is 60 per cent for activities under output 4, and the allocation of funds will vary by country, based on criteria such as local population, land use, poverty levels, and other factors that are relevant to the project implementation cycle. The blended finance facilities (output 7) anticipate financing up to EUR 150,000 per project component, with a maximum of three projects per beneficiary across the four countries. The distribution of GCF resources across the four participating countries is expected to be fairly even, as shown in table 4.

Table 4: Distribution of GCF resources by country

| Country | Estimated GCF funding (EUR) | Overall resource envelopes by local governments | Target local governments |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Burkina Faso | 10,874,322 | 12,563,179 | 20 |
| Côte d'Ivoire | 12,400,000 | 14,000,000 | 20 |
| Mali | 10,556,628 | 12,674,975 | 20 |
| Niger | 13,645,672 | 15,790,518 | 20 |
| TOTAL | 47,476,622 | 55,633,702 | 85 |

Source: Annex 17 to the funding proposal

The funding proposal allows for performance monitoring and safeguards to be put in place to ensure that GCF resources are used for the purposes intended. The PRGF allocation to local governments will be subject to a condition precedent related to the country's progress concerning good governance and public financial management, and a performance-based element (30 per cent) based on scores received during annual monitoring assessments.

The use of funds under component 1 is expected to channel GCF funding directly to local authorities. For the blended finance facilities, UEMOA CCF will act as the regional EE on grant funds that are selected for each country, within the framework of the national steering committee.

The TAP finds that the process for identifying and selecting country-level microfinance institutions (MFIs) under blended financing facilities is complex. It recommends potential partners with adequate capacities, standards, reach and interest in climate adaptation finance will be considered as part of the selection criteria. The TAP also notes that MFIs are important actors, especially in the context of the proposed programme set-up, where these institutions will have to play a key role in selecting, structuring and monitoring adaptation and mitigation investments. It is recommended that the TAP consider the inclusion of a condition precedent to first disbursement under the Funded Activity Agreement between GCF and UEMOA CCF.

The governance arrangements (see figure 4) are complex, and it is not clear how decision-making will be coordinated across the separate technical committees for the two financing instruments. The TAP recommends that the TAP consider the potential impact of the PRGF mechanism and the blended financing facilities, which could lead to inefficiencies of coordination, duplication or suboptimal utilization of GCF resources. In view of the TAP, it


---
**Page 101**
---

will be important to ensure that these two streams are managed coherently so that the potential of the local investments can be maximized and duplications reduced.

Figure: Governance arrangements

| | |
|---|---|
| **Funding proposal** | **Investment Framework (IF)** |

### Source: Funding proposal

#### a6.
While the programme outlines the vision for its long-term sustainability, its actual realization will largely be contingent on the extent to which the four countries institutionalize local climate finance through intergovernmental fiscal transfers, whether the private sector retains sufficient interest in the blended financing facility, and whether it is able to attract external funding mobilized from domestic and international sources to sustain the blended instrument. The programme should therefore include a clear description of the milestones, demonstration of proof of concept of the financing mechanisms, and emerging results could be important for building local ownership and could help to maintain the programme’s operations and impact.

#### a7.
Under the blended finance facilities, the funding proposal envisages a repayment rate of 70 per cent over a period of five years. The investment framework (IF) accompanying this funding proposal sets out strategies to manage these risks; regular monitoring will be critical to manage risks of non-repayment and ensure sustainability.

#### a8.
The exit strategy from the blended finance mechanism involves seeking new equity and loan guarantees to ensure sustainability. BOAD and UEMOA are currently looking for this additional financing, but no concrete commitments have been made yet. The funding proposal does not provide any evidence that there have been credible expressions of interest from international financial institutions.

#### a9.
In the view of the ITAP, the investment framework (annex 3A) lacks a crucial element—that of evaluating climate-related risks. The investment framework does not build in sensitivity to the specific needs and challenges related to disaster risk management (DRM), natural resource management (NRM), and maintenance (DM). Revenue generation or the overall cost of doing business. The AE has acknowledged that there will have to be a more integrated approach to incorporating climate risks into the financial assessments. The ITAP finds that it will be important to

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

#### II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

74 The funding proposal for the LaCGA mechanism is comprehensive. The institutional framework and PICRG financing schemes for local governments in all four countries are clearly articulated, with a strong focus on capacity building and learning from experience, similar to UNCDF in other countries. The TAP recognizes that while the blended finance facilities could be innovative in the four participating countries, there could be some associated risks as the progamme is new and there may be limited experience of using blended climate finance for adaptation action. Additionally, political and security risks remain a key consideration.

75 The TAP recommends that the AE undertake the following during project inception:
(a) Clarify how the governance mechanisms will enable coordinated management and accountability across the two financing mechanisms, namely the PICRG and the blended finance facilities, which will be essential to delivering a coherent programme (see para. 64 above).

(b) Develop and apply an investment framework that also factors in the climate risk of the proposed investments under different scenarios (see paras. 67 and 68 above).

(c) As intended by the funding proposal, ensure that the ACAF and M&K framework build in measures to track resilience improvement trajectories. This could also offer important lessons for other regions in the region that are also vulnerable to climate change (see paras. 70-72 above); and

The GCF Secretariat will review the funding proposal with the following conditions to be fulfilled (see paras 63 above) prior to the first disbursement of GCF proceeds.

Conditions precedent to the first disbursement of GCF proceeds under the funded activity include:

(i) A detailed list of all microfinance institutions ("MFIs") that have been identified as potential participants in the programme across the host countries;

(ii) A detailed description of the process that the Accredited Entity will follow to select MFIs for inclusion in the programme, including criteria used by the host countries; such process to ensure transparency, inclusiveness, and be aligned with the programme's objectives of fostering climate resilience at the local level;

(iii) An analysis of the capacity of each MFI to provide the services required by the MFLs, such criteria to include financial capacity, alignment with climate adaptation and mitigation projects, access to credit for small enterprises, and capacity to support local businesses and vulnerable communities; and

(iv) A short report confirming that new consultations have been undertaken with local governments in the host countries to ensure that the selection model for MFIs is sustainable and supports the objectives of the programme.

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment

## Proposal name:
Local Climate Adaptive Living Facility Plus (LoCAL+) - West Africa (Burkina Faso, Ivory Coast, Mali and Niger)

## Accredited entity:
Banque Ouest Africaine de Développement (BOAD)

## Countries:
Burkina Faso, Ivory Coast, Mali, Niger

## Project/programme size:
Medium

### Impact potential
To maximize sustainable development outcomes, Microfinanced institutions will be selected according to the specifications. This will take into account their geographical position, experience, local deployment capacity, as well as their compliance with financial standards in the WAEMU region, overseen by the Confederation of Microfinance Institutions. Funded projects will be implemented through a participatory and inclusive approach based on the Climate Change Adaptation Framework approach. The terms of reference will be available from the start of the project implementation phase.

### Paradigm shift potential
The LoCAL methodology is already proven, tested and performance-based (through annual assessments and capacity building), with selection criteria ensuring rapid and effective results even for small-scale projects. The Local Information System for Adaptation (LISA) is an online platform that provides shared access to climate information, enabling local governments and communities to make informed decisions on adaptation planning and budgeting. The Local Information System for Adaptation enables participatory feasibility studies and the development of community-based adaptation strategies. The blended finance facility will mobilize funding to help communities in: a) adapting to the effects of climate change by improving their resilience; b) reducing damage and losses to assets and infrastructure; and c) making income streams less vulnerable to the effects of climate change.

### Sustainable development potential
N/A

### Needs of the recipient
N/A

### Country ownership
All stakeholders were thoroughly consulted. All stakeholders have been thoroughly consulted. At the beginning of the project, a consultation and information workshop will be held, followed


---
**Page 105**
---

by a start-up report to be submitted to the GCF Secretariat. This approach will gather updated information to enhance project implementation.

### Efficiency and effectiveness

The accredited entity will focus on the careful selection of Microfinance Institutions based on a comprehensive set of criteria, ensuring their capacity to manage climate investments. The Regional Management Unit (RMU) will provide technical assistance for this process. The sustainability of the blended finance facility will be guaranteed by the technical and financial commitment from the RMU. Capacity building activities, including training and workshops, will help Microfinance Institutions integrate climate finance into their business models. Additionally, BMAD and its financial partners (AFD, KFW, EIB) will offer climate rehabilitation services to support Microfinance Institutions in the region.

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The institutional framework will be organized around a Regional Management Unit (RMU) responsible for coordinating the activities of the four (4) National Management Units to ensure synergy between the two financing instruments (Performance-Based Climate Resilient Grants and the Blended Finance Facility) at regional and national levels. An investment framework that accounts for climate change adaptation needs will be developed, with a focus on resilience. The framework will be supported by monitoring and evaluation using the Assessing Climate Change Adaptation Framework.

The Local Development Agency (LDA) will provide technical assistance to Microfinance Institutions in developing their project proposals and conducting financial planning for public and private stakeholders. A detailed list of Microfinance Institutions and the selection process will be provided to the GCF before the launch of sub-component 2.2

# FOREWORD

Women are not only the first observers but also the first victims of climate change by virtue of their roles in today’s society and responsibilities in food production, collecting firewood, freshwater, and water. They were the first to observe the decreasing agricultural productivity and depletion of water resources. They are the most vulnerable as, when climate change disrupts livelihoods, they are the most affected. Women are also more exposed than men to natural disasters such as floods, droughts, cyclones, and men. Women and children are more likely to die or be injured in disasters, specifically in countries where women have limited access to resources.

Women are also effective agents of change as they cope and adapt differently to men, and they play a preeminent role in the family.

Despite their vulnerability, women should not be seen only as victims of climate change; but they should also be considered as potential effective promoters of adaptation and mitigation. For example, women have historically developed knowledge and skills related to water harvesting and storage, food preservation and rationing, and other practices that can help them adapt to the effects of climate change. They are also key agents in promoting change projects to both empower women’s capacity and knowledge and provide them with the tools and skills they need.

The LOCAL COMMUNITY ADAPTIVE LIVING FACILITY PLUS (LUCAL+V) West Africa programme intends to reach the population of up to 85 local governments of Burkina Faso, Ivory Coast, Mali, and Niger and build the adaptive capacity of communities by contributing to reducing their vulnerability to climate change impacts. The initiative is designed with a focus on women’s empowerment as an essential element for vulnerable groups, women especially and can be expected to indirectly generate new job opportunities for both men and women.

The initiative will therefore seek to participate in enhancing the socio-economic empowerment of women at the local level for example by supporting the establishment of community contracting systems. The programme also aims at improving access and supporting access to finance and improved infrastructures. Throughout its design and implementation, it will maintain gender and social inclusiveness considerations so as, in particular:

(a) To ensure that the voice, needs and perspectives of all social groups have the opportunity and a real chance to be heard.

(b) To take into account the above perspectives and input in the design and delivery of all Programme activities.

(c) To avoid at all times any unintended negative repercussion of any Programme activity on any vulnerable group (i.e., women).

(d) To explicitly use this initiative as an avenue to foster wider participation and engagement in public governance by multiple stakeholders (incl. women).

The programme notably builds on the experience of the LUCAL mechanism, already implemented by UNICEF in Mali, Niger and Burkina Faso. LUCAL has an important track record in supporting good use of gender indicators and in promoting gender equality.

In partnership with UNIDO, it has notably had the opportunity to put the LUCAL approach to good use in the implementation of the project “Support for risk management aimed at environmental degradation through community-based adaptation” funded by the United Nations Environment Programme’s Peace Building Fund. This project, implemented over an 18-month period, notably aimed to support local communities in Burkina Faso and Niger in addressing the risks associated with environmental degradation and climate change in 4 local governments of Mali.


---
**Page 108**
---

In terms of results, support to the local planning and enhanced management of natural resources at the local level as part of said program has had long term effects in terms of prevention of potential conflicts arising from their exploitation. The gender sensitive training provided to elected representatives and local authorities has helped them to better understand the importance of women’s participation and better serve vulnerable communities. In addition, the project has encouraged the selection of women's groups for revenue generating activities. These women's groups have been trained and supported to launch their own projects, which have had a positive impact on the economic empowerment of women, particularly the effects of climate change, and by helping target populations (notably women) to have more sustainable livelihoods in the face of environmental degradation.

In addition, women and young people have been systematically involved in consultation frameworks and training workshops. This has helped them to better understand their rights as well as their responsibilities, contributing to their involvement in the community. Communes have had their planning documents updated to include aspects of adaptation, environmental management, and gender and conflict sensitivity analysis. These changes have contributed to a more inclusive approach by communes to reduce potential conflicts and include women in their roles as actors in development and peace. The project has also helped to strengthen the capacity of women’s groups, adapted to respond to natural disasters, strengthened social cohesion and inclusion, rethink the implementation of certain communal interventions, and reduced/mitigated the risk of conflict.

These experiences with LIOCL have informed the design of the current proposal and are reflected throughout this document.


It deserves be noted that women have been consulted during the participatory process to develop the current proposal and will remain key stakeholders for engagement and consultation throughout the program implementation. # GENDER ASSESSMENTS

## BURKINA FASO

Summary: challenges of integrating gender equality into adaptation and resilience programmes in Burkina Faso.

Gender equality and women's empowerment is a priority development issue in Burkina Faso. By adhering to the Beijing Platform for Action (1995) for the promotion of women’s rights as well as the Sustainable Development Goals adopted by the United Nations in 2016, the country has committed itself to undertaking profound reforms and ensuring that conditions are conducive to the full participation of women, and to adopt policies, legislation and institutions that will make it possible to achieve gender equality.

The political will to integrate gender, climate change adaptation and resilience is also present in Burkina Faso. The National Adaptation Plan (NAP) for Burkina Faso (2015-2030) and the National Adaptation Plan – NAP (2015) is exemplary in this respect, placing gender at its heart and dealing with it in a cross-cutting manner, making a real effort to integrate it across the different sections (which is not the case of other countries). However, this political will does not always translate into concrete action and the gap with real-life experience remains wide. The diagnosis established by the Plan National de Développement Économique et Social (National Economic and Social Development Plan – PNDES), the reference framework for Burkina Faso’s development until 2015, shows that despite the progress made, considerable inequalities in poverty, education, employment, access to productive resources, health care, housing, land tenure, and access to credit still exist between men and women. Generally, despite the fact that gender equality is enshrined in the legislative framework, social norms and practices continue to be a major obstacle to the full enjoyment of human rights by women, whether in terms of access to resources, access to education, political participation or access to justice. Women’s living conditions and rights continue to be marred by various forms of exclusion and violence, as stipulated in the 2019 report on Burkina Faso's implementation of the Beijing Platform for Action (Government of Burkina Faso, 2019:2 years later).

In the area of disaster risk reduction and resilience to climate change, there is an emphasis on women’s vulnerability. The notion of gender often comes down to women as a vulnerable group with the same difficulties in accessing resources and exploring their capacities at all on climate change.

Another challenge that remains for the political will to address climate change adaptation points to the security situation in the north-east of the country and the internal displacement caused. This situation increases the vulnerability of women and girls and gives rise to an emergency logic: providing assistance to displaced populations as quickly as possible, rather than an integrated and long-term policy. As the events are recent, it is difficult to assess their magnitude and the impact they will have on national climate policy and action.

## Analysis – national level

### What are the national policies (and related issues? [include policy name, date])

- National strategies for strengthening the role of women in development (National Gender Policy) (2009)
- National Plan for Economic and Social Development (Plan National de Développement Économique et Social) (PNDES) (Government of Burkina Faso). The PNDES is a 15-year plan that aims to reduce poverty, improve the quality of life, and make women dynamic actors in development.
- Sectoral strategies with gender equality among their objectives: National Strategy for Accelerating Growth and Development Durable (Strategy for Accelerated Growth and Sustainable Development) (SAGD) (Government of Burkina Faso). Gender is one of the priority cross-cutting issues. The PNDES 2013–2017 includes a gender equality component.
- National Literacy Programme (National Accelerated Literacy Programme) 2013–2015 (Government of Burkina Faso).
- National Strategy for Accelerating Girls' Education (National Strategy for Accelerating Girls' Education) (Government of Burkina Faso).

### Programmes/action plans?

- National strategies for strengthening the role of women in development (National Gender Policy) (2009)
- National Plan for Economic and Social Development (Plan National de Développement Économique et Social) (PNDES) (Government of Burkina Faso). The PNDES is a 15-year plan that aims to reduce poverty, improve the quality of life, and make women dynamic actors in development.

### Who is the governmental body (Ministry/Agency/Office?) in charge of gender issues?

- Ministry of Women, National Solidarity and Family Development
- National Committee for the Fight Against the Practice of Female Genital Mutilation
- In addition, gender groups, institutional and ministerial commissions, and inter-ministerial committees are in place at all levels, including at the level of ministers, although their capacities need strengthening (Japan International Cooperation Agency [JICA] 2015).


---
**Page 111**
---

- A national gender observatory is housed at the National Institute for Statistics and Demography (INSD) and is responsible, among other things, for defining monitoring indicators.

Are gender-disaggregated statistics available?
- Yes – in 2015, the INSD produced four “Tunets Genre” three-booklets: Three booklets on themes similar to those of the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs): living, employment and vocational training, violence and crime, governance and decision-making.
- The booklets also provide gender-disaggregated data on themes similar to those of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): water supply and sanitation, poverty, sustainable development and environment, health, education, and security.

National databases such as the World Bank’s Gender Data Portal (https://datacatalog.worldbank.org/indicator?cid=10358) also provide gender-disaggregated data on Burkina Faso. The database includes indicators of sex-disaggregated and/or gender-sensitive data.

What is the legal status of women in the country of intervention (please specify policy / Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW)/Bridging of international law)?
- The Constitution of 15 June 1991 prohibits discrimination, including that based on sex, language, religion, caste, political opinion, fortune and social status.
- Customary law is recognized by the Constitution as a source of law similar to “customs as accepted and brought into force in accordance with the Constitution.” With regard to inheritance, the Persons and Family Code (PFC) provides that women have equal rights with men to inherit property. In practice, customary law prevailed over statutory law, which meant that women had to ask the family of the deceased husband to appropriate the couple’s property. The PFC also provided for a woman’s right to marry well and children. Article 15 of the Constitution guaranteed the right of women to education. However, in practice, traditional reform gave women equal access to land, yet in practice they still had limited rights.
- The Persons and Family Code establishes that spouses are equal partners in all aspects of family life. It also provides authority, responsibility and obligations towards the children. In case of divorce, the court decides on the place of residence; the right of decision rests with the husband (article 248). Women have a right to choose their profession without the consent of the husband provided that this profession is not contrary to public order and morality (article 305).
- Article 196, in law has prohibited female genital mutilation (FGM); however, in practice, it persists.
- The law was amended in 2009; it establishes that 80 per cent of candidates for local elections must be women.

Do women have equal access to technical knowledge and information about their educational status of girls? And boys? Adult literacy is disaggregated by sex?

The proportion of girls among all primary school pupils has increased from 46 per cent in 2008/2009 to 51.3 per cent in 2017. Over the period 2008–2015, the gross enrolment rate at primary schools for boys and girls was 95.7 per cent (page 64) for boys and from 67.7 per cent to 81.4 per cent for girls (ibid.).

Between 2009 and 2015, the proportion of girls in total access to education increased from 43.1 per cent in 2008/2009 to 76.7 per cent in 2015.

During the period 2009–2015, the proportion of girls who did not attend school due to pregnancy or childbirth decreased from 48.2 per cent to 44.1 per cent (ibid.).

The gross enrolment rate at secondary schools for boys and girls increased from 63.7 per cent in 2009/2010 to 75.5 per cent in 2017.

In the north, girls are more likely than boys not to attend school due to pregnancy or childbirth (ibid.).

The gross enrolment rate at tertiary institutions was 52 per cent for men and 28.9 per cent for women (Social Dashboard 2017).

What are the main issues for accessing justice and the barriers that women face?

The stigma attached to violence prevents victims from seeking help to end it. Only 4 per cent of female respondents in rural areas highlight that and 42 per cent of male victims of domestic violence sought sufficiently effective assistance. Women who have experienced violence are more likely to confide in their friends (68 per cent) or family (38 per cent) than in police officers (10 per cent). The main reason for not seeking help is the fear of retaliation. Only 24 per cent of women who were victims of domestic violence sought assistance from a professional (such as a doctor, lawyer, social worker), and only 56 per cent of men did so.

The objective education provided by family planning programmes is insufficient to change people’s attitudes towards the role of women in society. The National Gender Policy discloses a need “to work to strengthen the legal culture among the people, and to ensure that they are aware of their rights and obligations.” Justice” In short, understanding and acceptance of the law, as well as the ability of women and other actors themselves, must be heightened. A favourable environment for the implementation of gender mainstreaming assistance, must therefore be created” (National Gender Policy, 2018).

In practice, women’s ability to access justice and to bring their cases before a court is hampered by many factors such as lack of information on their rights, legal knowledge, financial resources, language barriers, illiteracy, complexity of legal procedures and other obstacles. The Committee is concerned that enforcing court rulings has been slow in some regions. It also notes that although the State party has carried out a reform to the justice system, it still lacks sufficient financial and human resources for its effective implementation” (CEDAW, 2018).

What are the current indicators and trends related to women’s development? (human development index world rank? Groot)

HDI: 0.735 (ranked 139 countries and territories)
UNDP report: 1,600 (2013) (ranked 2018).
GDP per capita: $3,600 (2013) (ranked 2018).

**Page 117**

At the national level, the net activity rate according to the International Labour Organization (ILO) is 58 per cent for men against 71.8 per cent for women, a difference of 7.1 percentage points. Women's economic participation is lower than that of men, and this gap has widened over time: in Burkina Faso only 42 per cent of Burkinabé women were active in 2016 compared with 65 per cent of men (ILO, 2018). This has increased since 2006 (ILO, 2018) and is consistent with the gender gap indicator.

In 2015, women accounted for only 24 per cent of self-employed workers in Burkina Faso. According to the Centre Régional d'Information et de Formation en matière de Droits des Femmes (CRIFDF), Women's Empowerment and Information and Training in Africa – CRIIFD (n.d.), women's access to financial resources is still very limited, especially for those who are economically active. This labour force is mainly composed of family workers (35 per cent) or unpaid family workers (40 per cent). In some cases, women are self-employed and work in the informal sector (19 per cent), but this figure has increased since 2016 (15 per cent) (OECD, 2018). The same report speaks of a "gender gap" in terms of access to financial resources for entrepreneurship, with a strong overrepresentation of men among entrepreneurs.

The Global Gender Gap Report (2013) gives Burkina Faso 0.77 on gender pay inequality, where 0 represents perfect equality and 1 represents complete inequality.


What is the situation in terms of access to financial resources? Only 4 per cent of women have a bank account compared with 18 per cent of men; 12 per cent of women and men have savings accounts (source: World Bank Group, 2017).

Information on the number of women benefiting from microfinance projects or self-help groups were not found. However, it is known that since 2015, when the government has created funds to enable women to access credit for agricultural activities, the Association of Active Women Farmers (FAGIRA) and the Support for Agricultural Microcredit Activities (SAMAC) have been set up.

These funds, directly managed by government structures, provide microcredits or microloans. However, microcredit, not collateral, on the basis of a third-party guarantee, remains the most common form of financing, especially women and youth, remains a challenge for the National Fund for Agricultural Development (FAND).


Article 15 of the Constitution guarantees the right to property for all of the agricultural and land tenure reform/Deutscher-Stiftung für Entwicklungszusammenarbeit (DSE) continues to have little access to land (IFAD, 2016). The regional development strategy (RDS) provides regional development strategies for each region. However, many regions are often excluded from disaggregated budgeting and financing mechanisms such as the Land

# Climate and gender in the proposed project/program footprint area

## at the national level

### Guiding questions:

#### Gender equality – national level

- The gender dimension is not very visible in Burkina Faso's NDC (2015). It is only mentioned once, in the context of cross-cutting themes, including the Ministry for the Promotion of Women and Family Solidarity.
- The NAP (2015) pays more attention to this issue and uses vulnerability assessments as a tool to identify gender gaps across sectors and for different social groups. Gender mainstreaming is one of its main strategies. However, the NAP does not mention specific actions for greater relevance and effectiveness in addressing gender equality.
- To address the gender gap identified in the NAP requires the participation of men and women in actions for greater relevance and effectiveness in addressing gender equality.

#### What are the governmental bodies (Ministry? Agency? Office?) in charge of these issues?

- Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock, Forestry, Energy and Infrastructure
- Ministry of Food and Nutrition Security, Water Resources, Physical Planning and Environment
- Ministry of Intended Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC), sustainable land management, climate change adaptation

#### What are the priority sectors targeted by climate-related NAPs and NDC?

- Pillars of sustained agricultural growth: agriculture, livestock, forestry, energy and infrastructure.
- Food and nutrition security, water resources, physical planning.

#### Are there national programs addressing each climate change goal, and are they implemented independently or in an integrated manner?

- Africa Hydrology Program - Strengthening Climate Resilience in Sub-Saharan Africa
  - Burkina Faso Country Project includes a Gender Action Plan (GAP)
  - Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS) Policy for Water Resources Management (2015)
  - The gender dimension is taken into account in national climate action plans and NDCs.

**Page 119**
---

# How does the government frame both issues?

- **Vulnerability of women:** "People who are weak like women" (Worldwide National de Décentralisation Rurale et Rural (National Rural Land Security Policy), 2015).
- **Women are more vulnerable to climate change than men:** "The most vulnerable groups are among the rural poor, women, youth, small-scale farmers and pastoralists" (UNDP, 2013).
- But the important role of women in development and the need to strengthen their participation and capacity is also recognized (RAP, 2015).

# Are there any policies or initiatives referring to gender equality?

## Initiatives both adaptation and mitigation?
### Any background studies?

Project 1: Training/information/awareness raising for women's associations
- Strengthen the initial literacy of women members of women's associations in areas such as water harvesting, rainwater harvesting, gardening, wood energy, traditional pharmacopoeia, household waste management, food preservation, processing of forest products, agriculture and livestock.
- Facilitate access among women for effective participation in local governance.
- Awareness raising and skills capacity in gender equality and women's leadership.

Project 2: Strengthening the capacities and ability/capacity of women associations to climate change through the implementation of income generating activities
- Strengthen the capacities of women's associations in running income-generating activities for women's empowerment.
- Ensure that irrigation water levels at market gardening sites are ensured by using appropriate equipment and technology.
- Improve nutrition among the most vulnerable groups (especially women) by improving food security, access to nutritious foods, and quality of products.
- Equip women with appropriate equipment and technology to carry out their activities.
- Increase income from market gardening production to contribute to food security.

Project 3: Research on good adaptation practices that benefit and reach women (RAP, 2015)
- Integrate traditional knowledge into research strategies to improve women's climate change adaptation practices.
- Mainstream appropriate technologies that are less physically constraining and less costly to implement.

National gender-sensitive and sustainable second-generation regional and communal development plans.

# Are there international organizations supporting the government in implementing these initiatives?

- Burkina Faso has received financial support from Denmark, Japan, Global Environmental Fund (GEF) and UNDP to support the Burkina Faso NAFOR project (since 2012).


---
**Page 120**
---

- Capacity building on adapting and reducing vulnerabilities to climate change in Burkina Faso: Programme of Action National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) BMU/UNDP/Burkina Faso International Development Agency (DANDA)
- Adaptation to climate change for improved human security in Burkina Faso [BMU-BEF-UND-PANDA]

### Are there background studies both adaptation and gender?
- Sophie Rig, Emma Lovell, Florence Pitchon (2015). Assessing gender in resilience programming. Burkina Faso
- Chantal Koffi, Emma Lovell, Women Farmers in Burkina Faso: Impact and Adaptation Policies and Practices. Burkina Faso.
- Chantal Koffi, Emma Lovell, Gender and Climate Change in Burkina Faso: Subnational study, focus on the Kénédougou region. Burkina Faso
- Florence Pitchon, Sophie Rig, Camille Audin, Alexander Ritchie, Mark Pelling, Emma Vroman (2017). Building resilience by challenging gender inequalities: a gender transformative approach in BURKINA (Subnational study)

### What do available studies about the anticipated differences in men's and women's vulnerability and adaptive capacity to climate change?
- Women are less educated and have less access to the means of communication through which climate information is transmitted (Rigg, Lovell & Pitchon 2015). This makes them more vulnerable to climate change impacts.
- Women have limited access to land, water resources and technologies that could facilitate their work on the land (Ofdam, 2011). Women's vulnerability to food insecurity, therefore less influence on adaptation strategies (Rigg, Lovell and Pitchon, 2015).
- During periods of food insecurity, women are less likely to migrate than men. This is because they have more responsibilities in the household such as childcare, cooking and cleaning.
- Their economic situation is also worse: they earn lower wages or no wages at all; they do not have access to credit facilities and cannot obtain paid employment makes them less dependent on natural capital than men (Ofdam 2011). Women's vulnerability to food insecurity, therefore less influence on adaptation strategies (Rigg, Lovell and Pitchon, 2015).
- Their economic situation is also worse: they earn lower wages or no wages at all; they do not have access to credit facilities and cannot obtain paid employment makes them less dependent on natural capital than men (Ofdam 2011). # Summary: challenges of integrating gender equality into adaptation and resilience programmes in Côte d'Ivoire

The strategic vision in the Côte d’Ivoire National Development Plan (PDP) 2010-2020 calls for better integration of gender and environmental issues into public policies. There is a decidedly budgeted deficit for this. However, there is as yet no clear implementation of this vision. Despite there being a directorate responsible for gender equality in the Ministry of Agriculture, Environment, Water Resources, Family, and Children, it lacks sufficiently competent staff supported by an adequate budget to monitor or evaluate the progress made on gender equality issues. The 12 sectoral units that were rebranded in 2015 to support sectoral policies in each ministry have not been maintained and are now almost entirely non-existent (with the exception of the gender unit in the Ministry of Agriculture, Environment, Water Resources, Family, and Children). These units are underfunded (Ministry of Agriculture, Environment, Water Resources, Family, and Children) and inadequate due to a lack of human capacity and budget.

Gender is clearly seen as a donor priority and therefore an opportunity to receive funding. It is in this context that the National Gender and Climate Change Strategy embeds the National Climate Change Programme (NCCP) to confirm the State’s willingness to integrate gender issues into efforts to mitigate


---
**Page 125**
---

and adapt to climate change. However, as there is wide disparity in knowledge and understanding of the concept between different government representatives, the term "gender" is being superficially integrated into policy documents and project proposals to make them more "gendered". This is contributing to a gap between the commitment to gender mainstreaming shown by various stakeholders in adaptation initiatives and the effective translation into programmes that work to promote gender equality across all sectors and at all levels.

There are few examples of policies where gender issues are integrated and documented in detail. Many decisions makers sum up gender as women's issues and give examples of one-off projects where women have been involved, such as the project on climate change adaptation for women, particularly during International Women’s Rights Day, on 8 March each year. A notable exception is the work carried out by the Fonds Interprofessionnel pour la Recherche et le Conseil Agricole (Interprofessional Research and Agricultural Council) in France, which has created an Agricultural Gender Platform to bring together all actors involved in gender equality issues within agriculture. This platform could be a valuable asset in determining the scientific studies to be conducted in the agricultural sector, and the consultations to be carried out to better understand the various issues in climate change adaptation.

It will be essential to generate knowledge that is better informed about current inequalities in Côte d'Ivoire, as well as the potential for gender equality in the context of climate change. There is a clear need for scientific studies that document the impacts of current and future climate change on women’s rights and access to resources. The National Development Plan (NDP) 2016-2020 provides for better integration of gender into climate-related development decisions.

The 2009 National Policy on Equal Opportunities, Equity and Gender Equality in Côte d'Ivoire also refers to the need for women’s equal participation in decision-making processes and equitable access to and control of resources. The policy also refers to eradicating discrimination, including violence against women. This document is a basis for the Government’s commitment towards the Sustainable Development Goals in African Countries, compiled by the African Development Bank.

It is important that commissions for studies communicate clear requirements on integrating gender into research methodologies (for example, through terms of reference that require gender analysis in each study and project proposal). The National Development Plan (NDP) 2016-2020 provides for better integration of gender into climate-related development decisions. Studies, project proposals and logical frameworks for monitoring and evaluation must reflect gender considerations.

The document also refers to the need for women’s equal participation in decision-making processes and equitable access to and control of resources. The policy also refers to eradicating discrimination, including violence against women. This document is a basis for the Government's commitment towards the Sustainable Development Goals in African Countries, compiled by the African Development Bank.

- Yes, but only in reports by international organizations and in some sectoral development policy documents.

What is the legal status of women and children in the country of intervention (existing gender responsive legislation)? Ratification of Recommendation (CEDAW) - Ratification level? The country is ranked 172 out of 184 countries in the United Nations (UN) Gender Inequality Index (GII) under the United Nations Development Programme [UNDP], (2017). Karnataka was the first jurisdiction on the elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (EAW) in 1995. The Constitution of Côte d'Ivoire provides for the equality of all citizens before the law and prohibits discrimination based on sex, race or religion. Equality is enshrined in the Constitution of 8 November 2016 and applied in various laws such as the Law No. 2013-497 of 15 October 2013 (on political participation and combating violence against women) [UNDP, (2017)]. Customary law is recognized as a valid source of law by the Constitution of Côte d'Ivoire. New laws since 2013 more favourable to gender issues: law on marriage that obliges spouses to manage the household jointly; law on compulsory education for children and adolescents; law on equal access to public service status and labour code. Women are underrepresented in decision making positions in the Administration. They are a very small minority in elected positions, even though they represent 40% of the total number of voters registered on the 2010 electoral lists (World Bank). Only 11.4 per cent of parliamentarians are women (in 2018). Only 5 out of 36 ministers and 7 out of 90 members of the National Councils of State and Regional Councils has a female President (3.2 per cent). Moreover, of the 30 ministers appointed by the government on 4 January 2017 to 8 July 2018, only six were women (20 per cent). In State institutions, women represent 56% of civil servants and 29% of Ministers, and 12 per cent of Ambassadors are women [Ministry for Women]. Law No. 2019-870 of 14 October 2019 promotes the representation of women in elected assemblies.

In the review of the Beijing Declaration 20 years later, the government has recognized that gender equality is a human right and a fundamental human rights principle, to break down resistance and discrimination. The government recognizes that positive discrimination or affirmative action is often necessary. The Beijing+25 report summarizes the five main obstacles to equality: - lack of political will and insufficient funding
- weaknesses of funding mechanisms
- persistent socio-cultural barriers
- lack of coordination and insufficient dissemination of legislation


---
**Page 128**
---

- social norms and lack of ownership of the Gender and Development approach
- There are two known failures in the achievement of gender equality and women’s empowerment: (i) failure to improve the representation of women in decision-making positions, as was envisaged by the Fourth World Conference on Women in Beijing; (ii) lack of a mechanism for holding governments accountable to international authorities in the area of gender and women's empowerment (Ministry for Women, 2015).
- Young population with a high number of dependants: one out of two young people aged between 15 and 49 years old has three or more children under age 15 years (Republic of Côte d’Ivoire, 2016a).
- The health situation remains a cause for concern due to high mortality rates among women in reproductive age: the maternal mortality ratio is 378.4 per 100,000 live births (Côte d'Ivoire, 2015) and is among the lowest in the world (ADB, 2016).
- Women have equal access to education, technology, knowledge and information, including access to sexual and reproductive health services (disaggregated by sex)
- Access to education has improved, with an estimated primary school enrolment rate of 78.9 per cent in 2015 (UNICEF, 2016).
- The literacy rate among women is low: it is only at the level of primary schooling (a literacy rate of 54 per cent among men compared with 36.8 per cent among women (UN, 2006)).
- Prevalence rates for physical and sexual violence perpetrated by a spouse at least once since the age of 15 years old: 79.5 per cent (National Demographic and Health Survey - DHS, 2015).
- Prevalence rates for physical and/or sexual violence perpetrated by a spouse in the past year: 22 per cent (DHS, 2015).
- Early marriage is still common among girls aged between 15 and 19 years old, with the highest prevalence rate recorded in Côte d’Ivoire.
- Early marriages are prohibited by law but continue to be practised at a rate of 37 per cent (DHS, 2012): One third of girls between 15 and 19 years old have been married before their 18th birthday. They often drop out of school, almost all of them fall behind and find it difficult to follow up on their education. The situation is particularly worrying in schools in Côte d’Ivoire (Preparations in schools in Côte d'Ivoire, September 2015).
- The Penal Code prohibits sexual harassment (article 356) and rape (article 354: rape is punishable by life or 10 years imprisonment) (MFI, 2014).
- Lack of access to care for women victims of violence.
However, there are initiatives such as the decentralized legal clinics run in partnership with the Ministry of Justice and the National Bar Association.
- Access to legal aid is hardly ever free of charge. The victims themselves have to pay for it: they can benefit from the services provided by the Attribou – le Centre de prévention et d’assistance aux victimes des violences sexuelles (Attribou – Centre for Prevention and Assistance to Victims of Sexual Violence – PAVVS). Centres attached to the Ministry for Women – there are no funded shelter and relief facilities for women and girls who have been victims of sexual violence, except for psychological and counseling.
- There is not enough measures established for the perpetrators of violent practices and acts against women such as genital mutilation.


---
**Page 129**
---

What are the current indicators and trends related to poverty and development (e.g., HDI World rank/GDP per capita)?
According to the latest Human Development Report, Côte d'Ivoire ranks 1719 out of 188 countries in the low human development category in 2015 (UNDP, 2016).

Division of labour: Women work primarily in self-sufficient gardening, farming and animal husbandry (e.g., cocoa, coffee, palm oil, bananas and other crops) while men work in commercial agricultural production. According to the International Labour Organization Agency (ILOCA), 2013). Nevertheless, women have a strong presence in all economic activities, especially in agriculture, where they represent nearly 55 per cent in trade and 95 per cent in services (INS, 2015).

What do national statistics/information say about women's economic situation (employment rate? Average wage? Gender pay gap)? Any data on gender differences in access to resources?
The majority of the poor are women: the rate of poverty for women is 48.3 per cent compared with 39.7 per cent for men. The vast majority of women aged 15-64 years are economically active, compared with only 20 per cent of men (INS, 2015).

Women's wages are the equivalent of $125 per month, compared with $280 for men in urban areas and $379 in rural areas (UNDP, 2016).

Combined unemployment rate (i.e. taking into account underemployment) is estimated at 40 per cent for women and 30 per cent (Agence d'études et de promotion de l'emploi [Agency for Employment Promotion], 2015).

What is the situation in terms of access to financial resources for men and women in the areas where projects will be implemented? Savings account? Self-help group?
Women have unequal access to credit due to lack of collateral and reduced capacity to save and invest. Microcredit projects are potentially beneficial, but they are not always available (Agence d'études et de promotion de l'emploi [Agency], 2014).

What is the situation in terms of access to land? Property rights?
The rate of female literacy in Côte d'Ivoire is half as likely to complete secondary education as a boy. A woman, even with the same level of education as a man, has less chance of finding work and earning more money (World Bank, 2017). Only 30 per cent of women have access to credit from a financial institution, compared with 18 per cent of men (World Bank).

Within the Ministry of Solidarity, Social Cohesion and the Fight against Poverty, the Directorate for the Fight against Poverty is developing a policy to promote financial inclusion.

What are the rights, laws and policies for equal access to land between men and women, but in practice women in rural areas have difficulty getting access to land?
Unequal access to land and resources: the law in Côte d'Ivoire allows women to purchase land, but this right is only exercised in urban areas. In rural areas, women are not allowed to own or inherit land (INS, 2015). Only 8 per cent of women hold a title or certificate of sale, compared with 22 per cent of men. This difference, however, diminishes when customary law is taken into account (World Bank, 2017).

# Guiding questions

## Gender equality – national level

- The reference policy document for climate change is currently the one on National Development Strategies (NDS) of Cities of Hope. It incorporates adaptation strategies in the areas of Water Resources, Agriculture and Forestry, Health, and Disaster Risk Reduction/Disaster Management.
- Gender is not seen as a “sector” in the NDCs, without further detail on the implications it has for the vulnerability of the population or of certain sectors.
- The Education sector is not identified as a priority sector in the NDCs. However, education and health are now on the list of priority sectors for the NAP process.

## How are the national climate policies and strategic plans (NAP, NDCs) linked? (Include policy name, date)

### Mission of the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development:

- The Ministry is responsible for climate change issues under the supervision of the Ministry of the Environment and Sustainable Development.
- A gender and climate focal point within the NCSP was appointed in 2017.

## What are the governmental body (Ministry/ Agency’s) Office? or charge of the interface between these issues?

### NCSP:

- As part of the NAP process, the NCSP is aiming to develop climate change adaptation strategies focusing on the sectors targeted by the NDCs as being highly vulnerable: agriculture, access to water, and forest resources. These sectors are also those that have been previously identified as a sector of medium vulnerability.
- The forest sector is also linked with other interventions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (REDD+) Programme.

## What are the priority sectors targeted by climate change-related NAPs and NDCs?

### NCSP:

- Yes, the NCSP:
  - Gender issues will be cross-cutting for the priority sectors selected.
  - The NCSP has also commissioned a National Strategy on Gender and Climate Change (NSGCC) to be completed in early 2017.

## Are there national policies addressing climate change and gender inequalities independently or in an integrated manner?

### Examples of effective gender mainstreaming in practice include:
- The creation of the Agricultural Gender Platform by FAO in 2015, which aims at improving the effectiveness of agricultural projects to meet the interests of different socio-professional groups. Platform members are from government, civil society and private sector.
- The work for and with the support of the Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS) on gender equality in the energy policy of West African countries is another example of sectoral mainstreaming of gender issues.

### What do available studies say about existing gender roles and how these may be exacerbated by climate change impacts?

- Based on existing studies on the impacts of climate change in Côte d'Ivoire and West Africa, different gender roles in the agricultural sector are observed.
- Women play a significant role in agriculture, particularly in small-scale farming activities. They have different impacts on household livelihoods, income, and resources:
  - Reduced food yields will have a direct impact on women's ability to feed their families and thus on their main activity, but also along the value chain for food production and processing, which is dominated by women.
  - Little is known about the impact on the prices of raw agricultural products or how these changes may affect livelihoods along the value chains, or the alternatives available to men and women.
- Women's role in crop production will have a major impact on farmers' incomes, while water stress could affect both men and women differently. The consequences will be different depending on the crops affected by climate change. Women are more vulnerable groups to respond to change and to protect themselves or their families against its effects. Climate change will widely affect the activities of men, who are in the majority in agriculture, particularly in terms of land deforestation.
- Prolonged dry seasons and food insecurity are exacerbating the seasonal or permanent migration of men in search of employment opportunities. This makes it difficult to manage the day-to-day household needs for water, food, and energy.


---
**Page 133**
---

- The changes in temperature and rainfall that will impair crops such as cereal to move to higher altitudes will increase migration, pressure on land and potential land-related conflicts. These changes will also make people more vulnerable to land disputes between nomadic pastoralists and farmers, which can lead to intercommunity violence which will affect the well-being of both groups.
- Changes in temperature and rainfall also mean the use of water resources is likely to be affected by climate change with environmental impacts of which are poorly documented. The account of women’s role in agriculture is essential for understanding the constraints they face and the opportunities for improvement. Women on small agricultural units need to be assessed from a gender perspective, with attention given to awareness-raising and support.
- Changes in economic activity due to climate change and decreasing income of fishers will have an impact on those categories of people who are confined to certain roles or sectors, especially in an economic context where women’s participation is low. This applies both for men and women. Women has less access to productive employment opportunities than men, particularly in the informal and informal enterprises, which are characterized by low productivity and earnings. In 2015, only 38 per cent of women were employed compared with 64 per cent of men, while it is only 68 per cent for men (World Bank, 2017). Women’s access to productive employment opportunities is also lower than that of men in the formal enterprise or the public sector. In 2015, only 39 per cent of women were employed compared with 64 per cent of men.
- In addition to the impacts of climate change, anthropogenic activities are causing environmental degradation and destruction of coastal cities (movement towards Abidjan and San-Pédro) in particular, as well as the destruction of mangroves and coral reefs (Choive, 2015a). The degradation and destruction of mangroves and coastal forests for domestic farming or smoking of fish is particularly harmful to women. This is because women are more involved than men in these activities which are often associated with industrial pollution and overexploitation of aquatic resources are responsible for the loss of biodiversity, especially marine life, leading to irreversible reduction in biodiversity (International Performance Conference, 2015). The degradation of mangroves and coral reefs also has different social groups involved in these activities that are degrading the environment. These groups need to be identified and their needs addressed by the government to support environmentally sound resource management.
- Due to the lack of studies with a gender perspective, the differences between men’s and women’s vulnerability and capacities in relation to climate change adaptation are not well understood, and are not integrated into sectoral public policies.

imbalance in labour and time spent on domestic chores:

- Difficulties in water supply adversely affect women more than men: 78 per cent of households (89 per cent in rural areas and 56 per cent in urban areas) have no access to a safe drinking water source. - Women who are responsible for fetching water, access to water is crucial for their health and well-being as well as for adaptation to climate change. This means they have to devote a considerable amount of time to this task.

Women have higher exposure to vector-borne diseases, due to their domestic work with endemic health vulnerability in Côte d'Ivoire (malaria, schistosomiasis, filariasis, typhoid fever, respiratory infections, diarrhoeal diseases, malnutrition, etc., Republic of Côte d'Ivoire, 2014a).

- This contributes to increasing the risk of mortality from infectious diseases and limiting prevention strategies.
- The health system is characterized by an unequal national distribution of medical personnel. There are fewer doctors per capita concentration in the south of the country, particularly in Abidjan. Unequal division of productive and reproductive work: a large proportion of employment is in the informal sector. Women are more likely to be employed in jobs that do not benefit from social insurance (Republic of Côte d'Ivoire, 2014b).

- Women have unequal access to credit due to lack of collateral and limited financial education. Credit institutions are often male-dominated and potentially beneficial to women, but are limited in number [ARD].
- An adolescent girl in Côte d'Ivoire is half as likely to complete secondary school than a boy (Republic of Côte d'Ivoire, 2014b). Without education or a job, she finds it more difficult to find a paid job and earn an income.
- At the national level, only 17 per cent of women have a bank account in formal financial institutions, compared with 38 per cent of men (World Bank, 2013).

### MALI
Summary: Challenges of integrating gender equality into adaptation and resilience programmes in Mali.

Mal's official policies express a political will to mainstream gender issues in combating climate change. Mali's Third National Communication to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (2015) for example, includes a commitment to ensure that women have access to information and training of gender in other documents with more practical objectives, such as the Politique Nationale sur les Changements Climatiques (National Policy on Climate Change) [2013] and the National Adaptation Strategy [2011]. The political and legal frameworks for gender mainstreaming for adaptation and resilience are therefore present. However, there is a significant gap between these policies and their implementation in practice, which ones are still relevant, particularly following the disruption caused by the conflict since 2012.

In general, the gap between official policies and their implementation is significant. An emblematic case that illustrates this situation is the condemnation of the Malian State by the African Court of Human Rights in 2018 for failing to implement its obligations under international law on women's rights. This decision was endorsed by the Constitutional Court of Mali, which ruled that the State had failed to comply with its constitutional and legal obligations.


---
**Page 137**
---

State and civil society face when working together on these issues. The case of the African Court of Human Rights also reveals the reluctance of the Malian State to transform gender norms, citing the priority of maintaining peace and a desire not to fuel tensions in the country by maintaining the gender status quo.

The Malian government has made some progress toward equality for women. In fact, the legal equality envisioned in international documents, as evidenced by national statistics on the number of gender-based violence (GBV) cases, is there is a lack of resources to improve the situation.


Moreover, gender is treated separately, added to other non-homogeneous categories such as education and realization of rights. This creates a concern for complying with the expectations of international donors rather than a real desire for mainstreaming Gender. Gender is seen as a monolithic category, concerning women and girls, with an emphasis on their vulnerability (compared with that of young people) or their lack of access to resources, education, health services, employment opportunities, or at least the potential for them to do so. These observations must, however, be set against a civil society landscape that has been active for many years and various projects at the regional level.

What is the legal status of women and children in the country (existing gender responsive Convention on the Elimination of Discrimination Against Women and/or Ratification of international laws)?

Article 2 of the Constitution of Mali (1992) establishes equality between men and women, as well as their rights and duties. Any discrimination based on sex, race, religion or political opinion is prohibited.

The African Charter on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women and Girls and the African Charter on Human and Peoples Rights on the Elimination of Discrimination Against Women are ratified by Mali.

In practice, however, discrimination persists. In 2013, the Constitutional Court of Mali ruled that the African Court on Human and Peoples' Rights for non-compliance with the Maputo Protocol and CEDAW, first, because it is not possible to marry a woman under the age of 16 years (compared with 18 years for men) without her parents' consent; secondly, because the "consent" of the husband's parents (who are given by religious and customary law) is required before marriage can take place (for children); and third, for "violation of the obligation to eliminate discrimination against women that undermines the rights of women and children" (Afrobarometer, 2013; African Charter on Human Rights).

Female genital mutilation (FGM): 75.9 percent of girls aged 15–19 years and 75.1 percent of men aged 15–19 years think that FGM should be abolished in Mali (Afrobarometer, 2013). There are 11.5 times more male than female victims of FGM according to data from the EMOP survey, 2018.

### Climate and gender in the proposed project/program footprint area
> at the national level

#### Guiding questions
* **Gender equality – national level**
    * Cadre pour la Relance Économique et de Développement Durable du Mali (CREEDD) objective: "to promote inclusive and sustainable development for the reduction of poverty and inequality in a united and peaceful Mali, based on the principles of gender equality and social inclusion."
    * National Development Goals (DGDs) by 2030 - Sub-target 4.1.1: "Promote decentralized and participatory governance at all levels to ensure that women's voices are heard" and "put in place measures to improve women's control over productive land" and to "promote the participation of women in decision-making processes."
    * National Strategy for Gender Equality (2015-2030) - Sub-target 4.1: "Promote organizations, professional organizations, women and youth groups" (CREDD, 2016).
* **National Climate Risk Fund** takes into account the "quality and relevance of projects in terms of gender equality, social inclusion, environmental protection, gender, community involvement in implementation, etc." (UNDP, 2012).
* The gender mainstreaming section of Mali's Third National Communication to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) is included in the National Climate Change Policy accompanied by a National Strategy, both developed in 2015.
    * However, the National Strategy makes no mention of gender and women or the role they play in climate change adaptation. The Programme d'Action National (PAN) programme (le Programme de développement économique et social [Economic and Social Development Programme]) aims to "include women and young people in productive circuits" (2015).
    * The Programme d'Action National d'Adaptation aux Changements Climatiques (PANAC) of 2007-2016, which is part of the National Strategy for Climate Change (2007 aims "to strengthen intersectoral coordination by strongly involving institutions and organizations in the field of climate change" (Mali, 2008).
    * Plans d'Action Nationaux (National Action Plans - NAP) [Under development] are part of the National Strategy for Climate Change and are based on the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change. (2016).
