What are local governments responsible for vis-à-vis gender equity issues in development planning?

The MPEF has not been able to fully play its roles of influencing, coordinating and supporting the implementation of gender equality reforms that Mali has undertaken since 2000; strategic sections such as State reform, poverty reduction, health, education and rural development that are part of the National Strategy for the Acceleration of the Growth and Poverty Reduction Strategy Paper (GPRSP), have met with resistance from local governments. The MPEF was supposed to play these roles of policy influence and advice and that it intended to do so through its action plan and its implementation plan. Neither its action plan nor the configuration of its services were granted in this direction, which is why the MPEF has not been able to contribute to development (PNG, 2013).

The report of the Lead Development Countries Expert Group 2015 is broadly positive: "In several institutions established by the Government of Mali, women are present at all levels and in all areas of activity. The Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development and the National Council of Women have been created to promote gender equality."

"At the local level, the Climate Proofing Tool constitutes the main development tool of the municipalities. Developed in an inclusive and participatory manner with the participation of women and men and also takes into account specific aspects of women entrepreneurs."

The findings of the CREDS are more ambivalent: "Women are present in all levels of public administration, but they are often underrepresented or even overlooked when senior appointments are made. As a result, in most posts, women hold positions that are lower than those held by men and are rarely represented in the lists of candidates for the various elections. They are also under-represented at the level of local government. In addition to several women's associations and organizations such as the Coordination des Femmes de l'Etat et des Collectivités Territoriales (CFET), women economists, experts and former female parliamentarians and ministers, working to promote gender equality."

Similarly: "The issue of gender in governance concerns both the low representation of women at all levels of public administration and their responsibility to which women have access in the decision-making process. Elected representatives are not always aware that they represent a majority of women in public services, in the institutions of the Republic, on the political scene (6 women out of 105 deputies and senators), in local government bodies (2 men compared with 15 women) and in representation at the local level (85/147 mayors). The fact that women are under-represented in public service systems is also largely male dominated" (African Development Bank [AfDB] Group, 2013).

### The Project d'Autoconstruction Economique des Femmes dans le Fonds Ékrithe (Project for the Empowerment of Women in the Shea Subsector)

#### Background and Objectives

The project aims to empower women through economic empowerment with shea butter and eliminate economic inequalities. It is part of a larger strategy that includes strengthening technical and organizational capacities, increasing investment operations on one hand, and by increasing the technical and organizational capacities of women's associations. This plays a strategic role in the development of inclusive growth and employment. The project will be integrated into the national strategy to be incorporated into areas of intervention.

#### Implementation

A plan is in place to establish a monitoring system based on aggregated data and gender-related indicators. The programme will benefit 10,500 women directly (i.e., beneficiaries) and 287,000 women, i.e., an indirect beneficiary population of more than 800,000 people in the production areas of Kayes, Kédougou, and Ziguinchor (Adopted: April 19).

#### Support

To support this project, Legal Protection, 36 Bureau d'Assistance Juridique et Assistance Legale (Legal Assistance Office - BAJL) will be set up with the support of the NGO DEME. So in courts in the regions of Gaou, Kédougou, and Ziguinchor.

#### Two networks that should be included:

- **Fédération des Femmes Rurales du Mali (FFRM - Federation of Rural Women in Mali – FENEFEM):** This comprises nine provincial federations, 120 associations of rural women in the provinces, 730 communal associations of rural women in the cercles, and 703 communal associations of rural women in prospect. Its overarching objectives are "to improve the living conditions of rural women and fight against poverty," and its specific objectives are "to train and inform the members on health issues (especially malaria, tuberculosis, and malaria), develop production chains, build water supply points and promote the use of clean water, and provide training for women."

- **Réseau des Femmes Opérateurs Economiques du Mali (REFE):** The mission of this network is to "strengthen the economic and financial capacities of women's economic operators' organizations in Mali." Its specific objectives are "to develop entrepreneurship, trade, rural women and production, and finance." REFE has its headquarters at 10 rue de la République, Bâtiment R30. Phone: +221 (30) 29 29 09 / +221(76) 78 15 15 / +221 66 75 11 51 email: info@refe-mali.org

# NIGER

## Summary: Challenges of integrating gender equality into adaptation and resilience programmes in Niger

### Progress in gender mainstreaming in Niger

Unlike some African countries that have belatedly taken the issue of gender into consideration in national policies, Niger seems to have taken the marginalization of women seriously very early on (since 1996, in particular the Plan d'Intégration Genre et Inclusion Sociale [Gender and Social Inclusion Plan] PIGIS). The country has also been a pioneer in the field of gender equality, with the adoption of the National Gender Equality Strategy (2008-2017), the National Action Plan for the Promotion of Women's Rights and Citizenship (PAREC 2009-2015), the Sectoral Strategy for the Promotion of Gender Equality (SSPEG) 2016-2021, and the National Strategic Development Plan (PDES) 2017-2021; its constitution recognizing gender equality. etc.). Gender has become a cross-cutting issue in all the country's development policies. At the outset, this reflects an awareness of gender issues in the country, and also a strong will at very high levels of


---
**Page 151**
---

State, brought to life through the creation of the First Lady of Niger’s Foundation dedicated to women’s issues (Fondation Gur’i Ve Mellènje [Gur’i Ve Mellènje Foundation]).

In addition, Niger has acceded to most of the international conventions on African women’s issues (Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), Convention on the Rights of the Child) and several international institutions are developing programmes to meet the needs of girls and women in Niger. The World Bank carried out a study (Disrupting the Gender Divide, 2018) in four countries, including Niger, on climate change adaptation and gender equality. However, there are still significant challenges at the country level.

The main challenges for the country

The social norms, attitudes and beliefs of Nigerien communities contrast with the expectations of the national initiatives and policies implemented to promote women’s rights. Women continue to be regarded as second-class citizens in society and their access to basic services is poor. For example, women have limited access to legal and health education services; fields vital to physical, social and intellectual development in any society. The literature review also shows that gender inequality in terms of access to land, the main source of socioeconomic development in Niger.

The above challenges are also reflected in women’s economic situation: they are poorer than men, in other words, they have less access to income-generating activities. In 2014, only 35% of women were out of the 152 countries assessed. This shows that public policies on gender have not had the desired impact. These figures show that talking about gender means talking about social or societal transformation and not just about equality in terms of access to education or health care. It is clear that those who need to lead this struggle are both stakeholders and detractors (the institutions are in the hands of men, and men must use them to transform societies for the benefit of gender equality).

Gender equality in Niger will be achieved through a small “cultural revolution”. Men have control of the main resources and women do not have access to them. In addition, it is important that serious decisions in support of women are not taken unless there are institutions capable of leading this transformation. The lack of institutional capacity is one of the major obstacles to gender equality. This means that some decision makers from implementing different initiatives to support women’s rights, or at least to ensure that they are not a barrier to achieving gender equality on the local level. Original scientific research is needed to determine the feasibility of this transition (Issue 1).

Secondly, the documents consulted highlight a distinct lack of information on local (subnational) conditions. Niger is cosmopolitan (diverse in terms of languages, cultures, demographic dynamics, etc.). The main challenges and opportunities for women’s empowerment at national level are not necessarily those that differ at the regional level. Given this, it is essential to take actions specific to each region (by mobilizing those with knowledge of the areas and providing support in terms of capacity-building, etc.) (Issue 2).

Finally, the indicators related to education (Ministry of Planning, 2017) show women to also be at a disadvantage in this respect. Yet, access to decision-making positions in modern institutions, in a national or regional context, is not only about having knowledge. Therefore, comprehensive support (Issue 3) to promote women’s schooling, both at school and tertiary levels, would bring about major societal changes at the national and regional levels.

a - Country gender overall situation: rights and status of women in the country

Analysis – national level

* Politique Nationale de la Promotion de la Femme [National Policy for the Advancement of Women] (adopted by the Government in 1998)
* Loi interdisant les Mutations Génitales Féminines (MGF) (2003) [Law Prohibiting Female Genital Mutilation (FGM)] (2003)
* Plan d'intégration de la Gestion des Jeunes (National Youth Policy) (drafted in 2015)

* Sectoral strategic documents including the promotion of gender equality in their actions and objectives.
    * The Economic and Social Development Plan 2017–2021 (IDES) (Source: Profil Genre Niger, November 2016)
    * Programme Décentralisé de Développement de l'Éducation [Ten-year Education Development Decentralized Program] (IDES), 2003-2013 (Source: Gender Profile Niger, November 2016)
    * Demographic Health Survey (DHS) and Multiple Indicators (INDICMS DHS and Multiple Indicators) (2012)
* Politique Nationale de Genre [National Gender Policy – PNG] (2007), revised in 2013, adopted by the Government in 2015 [Source: Cadre National de la politique de Genre [National Gender Policy Framework]]

The formulation of the National Gender Policy was an opportunity to analyze the situation of gender in Niger and to locate the issue of equity and equal access for women and men. The policy is structured around four sub-themes that organize thematic issues (National Gender Policy, 2008).

Revised National Gender Policy (2017)

The National Gender Policy was revised nine years after its implementation to take into account the new demographic situation, population growth, along with peace, security and humanitarian emergencies. It is structured around the following four sub-themes:

1. Improvement of the sociocultural environment in relation to democracy, peace and security
2. Promotion of women's economic empowerment
3. Strengthening of the institutional and legal framework conducive to the effective implementation of the rights of women and girls, the fight against gender-based violence and the equal participation of men and women in power management


---
**Page 153**
---

3. Economic empowerment and inclusive growth in relation to environmental sustainability and climate change (in particular women’s economic empowerment)
4. Strengthening of institutional mechanisms and organizational frameworks for coordination, monitoring and evaluation and partnerships.

### Are gender-disaggregated data/statistics available?
- In 2013, the average annual growth rate was 3.9 per cent (the highest in the region) and 4.5 per cent (the lowest in the region), every two years.
- Women account for roughly 50 percent of the population, which is characterized by a median age of 18 years old; men account for about 50 percent of the population and their median age is 23 years old.
- Gender equality is promoted through gender parity in all economic, social and environmental sectors with the aim of reducing gender-based inequalities and disparities. The indicators used to measure progress towards gender equality and indicators from Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 5 are included in the PICS 2017–2021 [Results of Niger for the period of January 2017 – December 2021].
- Data on women’s participation in the World Bank Gender Data Portal (https://gdata.worldbank.org/indicator/cng) also provide gender-disaggregated data. In 2019, the proportion of female parliamentarians was 43% of the population of Niger; this proportion was used to calculate the number of female beneficiaries of the project in Niger.

### What is the legal status of women and children in the country (inventories/level of gender-responsive policies) in relation to the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW Ratification level)?
- While there has been progress in women’s participation in politics, the public sector and economic life, there are still significant challenges when it comes to representation. Yet the texts that make up the legal system in Niger do not provide a comprehensive framework for protection against gender-related violence.
- The country is party to several international conventions on human rights in general, and those, such as the CEDAW and the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights (ACHPR), which aim at promoting equality between men and women in particular. Although these conventions are now part of Niger’s legal system, they have not been fully implemented.
- On the one hand, to the failure to adopt additional domestic measures to support the implementation of these conventions; and, on the other hand, to the interpretations made by Niger’s courts. The CEDAW reservations render this convention meaningless and make adopting measures in compliance with it impossible for Niger.

- At the national level: article 8, paragraph 2, of the Constitution of Niger prohibits discrimination “on grounds of sex, race, social class, ethnic or religious origin”. Respect for the constitutional provisions is a duty incumbent on all public servants, including those in the judiciary. Under this law, the custom is applicable where it is consistent only with the principles of equality and non-discrimination between men and women; order and personal freedom, but also with the provisions of the international conventions ratified by Niger.


---
**Page 154**
---

- In the main text, the legislation and regulations in force recognize the same rights for men and women. However, in practice, the application of these laws is still, some of these texts deviate temporary measures (positive discrimination) or are not applied at all. This leads to a situation where there is no legal framework that protects women against certain violations of their rights (forced sterilization, female genital mutilation (FGM), sexual harassment, rape, slavery, prostitution, etc.)
- However, the coexistence of several types of law, with the effective dominance of customary laws and practices, often leads to a situation where the principle of equity in national legislation and international texts is ratified by the State.
- In the majority of these sociocultural environments, once they reach an age that allows them to marry (18 years old), women are expected to join the community, in forming matrimonial alliances and resolving family conflicts. However, this input does not mean that they have full authority in the final decision-making processes that affect the household or the community.
- Health problems: A high proportion of women in Nigeria. In Niger, more than 10% of women aged between 15 and 49 years old are infertile (source: INSD - Statistique (National Institute of Statistics – INS), 2017).
- The fertility rate is very high for women under the age of 35 years. For example, in the South-West, 68.3 per cent of women aged between 20 and 24 are pregnant.
- In the North-East, the fertility rate is even higher: 91% of women aged between 25 and 29 years old, compared with 24.2 years for men (INS, 2017). More than three out of four women (per cent) were already married/having a partner at age 18 or younger.
- In 2017, 36 per cent of married women between the ages of 15 and 19 were pregnant. This is a very high rate that can lead to serious health problems for young women, which often results in early pregnancies and the resulting complications, including maternal mortality.
- Although female genital mutilation (FGM) has decreased from 3 per cent in 2008 to 2.2 per cent in 2017, it is still practiced by some communities. For example, in the South-West of Nigeria, 65.9 per cent of women aged between 15 and 49 years old have undergone FGM. In the North-East, this stands at 12.8 per cent and 3.4 per cent, respectively (source: INSD – Statistique (National Institute of Statistics – INS), 2017).
- The situation is even more serious in some regions such as the South-West of Nigeria and Difa (Agence Régionale de la Presse (Nigerian Press Agency), 2016). In these areas, women are subjected to various forms of violence that perpetuate inequalities that affect their social status, limit their level of education and adversely impact on their health. This situation sanctions the precarities of women's reproductive health.
- The situation is even more serious in some regions such as the South-West of Nigeria and Difa (Agence Régionale de la Presse (Nigerian Press Agency), 2016). In these areas, women are subjected to various forms of violence that perpetuate inequalities that affect their social status, limit their level of education and adversely impact on their health. This situation sanctions the precarities of women's reproductive health.
- The situation is even more serious in some regions such as the South-West of Nigeria and Difa (Agence Régionale de la Presse (Nigerian Press Agency), 2016). In these areas, women are subjected to various forms of violence that perpetuate inequalities that affect their social status, limit their level of education and adversely impact on their health. This situation sanctions the precarities of women's reproductive health.
- A very high fertility rate with a total fertility rate of 7.3 children per woman on average. This is the result of early sexual activity and marriage (one in two girls aged between 15 and 19 years old are already married). Contraceptive use: In 2006, 11 per cent of women used contraception, of which only 3 per cent were using modern methods.
- The maternal mortality rate is also high, although it has declined slightly from 874 deaths per 100,000 live births in 2005 to 796 deaths per 100,000 live births in 2013. There are still significant disparities between regions and ethnic groups.
- High infant and child mortality rates, although there has been a marked improvement. The infant mortality rate fell from 121.1 per 1,000 in 1998 to 65.8 in 2013. Child mortality also decreased significantly between 1998 (174 deaths per 1,000 live births) and 2013 (118 deaths per 1,000 live births). In each category, male children have a higher risk of mortality than female children.

# What are the main issues for accessing justice and the barriers that women face?

## The most noticeable consequences are at the level of family law and personal status, where the legal regime is based on the customs or laws of the law, depending on the parties involved. While written law or statute law is in general patriarchal, customary law is essentially patriarchal in the most common interpretations – is unequal. In contrast to the situation for men, women are subject to a double standard of intergenerational transmission are always determined by gender and sanction subordination.

This situation leads to confusion about the status of women in the family. Under these conditions, women can neither make decisions on motherhood (birth or abortion) nor divorce their husbands without the latter's consent or at its dissolution.

The difficulty of establishing an effective legal order in a context of legal pluralism on the State's inability to intervene. This is compounded by the fact that women are often excluded from access to justice and different sources of law according to the interests at stake or the outcomes sought.


# What are the current indicators and trends related to human development? (Human Development Index (HDI) world rank? Gross Domestic Product (GDP) per capita)

## A high level of poverty: Economic growth appears insufficient to satisfy the basic needs of Niger's population given its rapid growth. This translates into a high level of poverty, with 70 percent of rural dwellers and 53 percent of urban dwellers and 15.5 percent of rural dwellers.

The economic structure is dominated by agriculture (62 per cent), livestock (58 per cent), Tibanou (56 per cent), Cissé (53 per cent), Tamaha (48 per cent) and Zinder (48 per cent). Economic activities are mainly carried out in the informal sector.

According to the National Survey on Housing Living Conditions and Agriculture – ECMAV (2011), the main determinants of poverty are: lack of access to land, education, health services and professional qualifications, lack of availability of productive assets in households and limited access to credit services.


The economic precondition for industrialization is weak. The primary sector, which accounts for more than 80 per cent of the working population, contributes only 25 percent of GDP (19.7 percent in agriculture and livestock sectors contribute 12 per cent and 37 per cent, respectively), (NIGER, 2010). This is due to a lack of competitiveness, a business climate not conducive to private sector development and the persistence of structural weaknesses in public financial conditions. Analysis of public finances also reveals inefficiencies in public spending and the need for better management of resources and the budget (Ministry of Planning, Land Use and Community Development, 2013).

- Although there are more women than men in the labour force, analyzing this according to gender shows that twice as many men work than women: The active rate for men is 60.1 per cent compared with 29.6 per cent for women. This means that while 58 out of every 100 men are working or actively seeking employment, more than seven out of ten (71 per cent) working women do not work in the formal sector.
- The proportion of households headed by a woman is 32.4 per cent. The composition of households does not seem to have a significant impact on the employment rate of women and men. Gender disparities according to place of residence are also significant: In urban areas, for every ten males there are seven females; in rural areas, for every five males can read and write (78.21 per cent) and 56.3 per cent, respectively, in rural areas fewer than three females for every ten males can read and write.
- In terms of health, despite the efforts made by the national authorities to improve access to reproductive health services, the situation regarding women remains critical and unacceptable. This position is characterized by a high maternal mortality rate (108 per 100,000 live births) and a low age at first marriage and of women's reproduction age in particular. This leads to high maternal mortality rates (46 per cent - DHS-MICS III 2006).

What do national statistics about women’s economic (employment rate? wage gap? Any data on gender differences in the agricultural sector?

The employment rate among the labour force is 41.43 per cent. In other words, barely four out of ten Nigerians are employed at any time during a given day prior to the survey, worked during this period. Analysis by gender shows that women’s employment rate was 28.57 per cent (a ratio of 60 per cent) and men’s 49.31 per cent (a ratio of 52 per cent). Women are more likely than men to be unemployed. Nigerians’ very low levels of employment reflect the economic activity at the national level (Genne et al., 2007; 2008) and Gender and Poverty, 2009–2014.

Rain-fed and irrigated agriculture is the primary occupation for Nigerien women. The agricultural sector accounts for about 35 per cent of GDP according to the Reassessment General Agriculture at the Chapter (IGAC) (NGA) 2006/General Agricultural Statistics, 2007. In 2014, the agricultural sector employed 89 million people and employed 13.3 per cent of agricultural households headed by males, and 7 per cent of those headed by females.

In rain-fed agriculture, women are involved in working in fields but at specific stages of the production process. In terms of irrigation agriculture, which is more productive than rain-fed agriculture, women’s participation varies according to the season; during the dry season, they are much more involved in marketing and building, including construction work. During the rainy season, women are more involved in marketing, gardening, or cooking.

The gender inequality index was 0.674 in 2016, placing the country 149th out of 153 countries on this measure. The GII is a composite index that measures education and reproductive health, and women’s participation in the economic sector.

In 2015–2016, the primary gross enrolment ratio was 70.2 per cent for girls and 89.4 per cent for boys; the secondary gross enrolment ratio was 39.8 per cent for boys and 39.8 per cent for boys. The parity index in primary education has remained stagnant at 1.05.

The analysis of the role of Nigerian women in the economy clearly indicates an uneven distribution of resources, with women having less access to land, credit, and other inputs as well as difficulties in accessing, managing, and controlling resources; unequal access to information and technology; and a lack of representation in decision-making at all levels. In the household economy: In the area of employment in particular, there is a low participation rate for women (18 per cent) compared with men’s participation rate (65 per cent). Women are also underrepresented in the labour force as a whole (25 per cent) for the male labour force (11.8 per cent) (Ministry of Planning, 2017).

The employment statistics indicate a low participation of women in the labour force and an unemployment rate twice as high (as 13.2 per cent). For as the labour force is defined as those who are employed or unemployed, it means that women represent 53.14 per cent of the total working population. Women’s employment rate was 28.57 per cent compared with men’s at 49.31 per cent. The fact that women carry out on a daily basis, being unpaid, are not considered as work and therefore do not appear in official statistics. These activities are essential for the renewal of human capital. Promoting women’s activities is also an important factor to increase the number of women in employment and improve the annual income parity per capita, which is 0.80 (National Gender Development Index).

Access to farming in the sector is also unequal because of the eligibility criteria (yield, productivity, input) which women find difficult to meet. In general, some women are excluded from agricultural activities as they are often involved in areas other than production. Men, on the other hand, often benefit more from the proceeds of their income-generating activities and from income-generating activities by women in the region seems very important. What is the situation in terms of access to land? Property rights?

- Soil and method of accessing land: The entire Department of Agualé is located on a single commune (commune d'Agualé). Access to land is mainly through inheritance or purchase, but there are also loans and payments. In Zabon (departmental commune), the majority of producers are aware of the price for one hectare plot ($100,000 = 150,000 FCFA). This exemplifies the problem of insufficient agricultural land in all departments. The average farm size is 2 hectares per person and 4.3 hectares (out of cultivable land), or 6 ha per person (Services d'Appui à l'Agriculture de la Région des Hauts Plateaux, 2015). This is confirmed by our surveys: the average farm employs ten (10) people and in April 2014, the average farm size was 3.8 hectares per person, with a range of 1.2 to 9.3 hectares per person, respectively.

- Access to land for women: Women are not allowed to own or inherit land; they can only use it as long as their husband is alive (100 percent). For the most part, women do not have access to credit and loans. In this area, it is difficult to access land for farming. The average farm size in April 2014 was 3 hectares per person at a rate of 20,000FCFA per hectare for one season. Producers say they have not yet begun to sell off parts of their land, so it would be difficult for them to determine a price for farmland.

# What are the priority sectors targeted by climate change-mitigated NAPs and NDCs?

- For the NAP, the highest priority is desertification control and natural resources management.
- The Niger government has prioritized adaptation options to be prioritized that those provide the best co-benefits in terms of climate change mitigation. These include: (i) the promotion of agroforestry; (ii) the use of improved seeds; (iii) the good adaptation practices and techniques that enable more carbon sequestration.
- These adaptation options are already well defined by existing strategies and action plans, such as the National Strategy for Food Security and Nutrition from the Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth Strategy (SDEC)—Niger 2018–2035; the National Plan for the Development of Agriculture in Niger (PNDAG); the National Climate Change Policy (PNPC), the Strategic Action Plan on Climate Change and Adaptation to Climate Change (SAPCCAC) and Action Plan on Climate Change and Variability (SPA-CCV).

# How does the government frame both issues?

The gender inequality index was 0.614 in 2018, placing the country 145th out of 153 countries. This reflects a lack of access to education and reproductive health, and women's participation in the economy.

At the same time, Niger is grappling with a vulnerable environmental situation due to climate change. This includes increased frequency and intensity of droughts, difficulties in sustainable management of land and water resources, and limited adaptation capacity at all levels of society, including households, communities, and governments.

The Government’s support for these two issues involves implementing the PDSI (2017–2021), which is based on most of the 17 SDGs. These include goals related to gender equality and women's empowerment (SDG 5) and resilience in the face of climate change (SDG 13). Niger intends to improve resilience and adaptive capacities in the face of climate change by increasing awareness-raising and individual and institutional capacities with regard to climate change.

The policy of reducing inequalities (SDG 10) involves the steady and rapid improvement of access to education, health services, and economic opportunities as well as the empowerment of all vulnerable people and their social, economic and political inclusion (UNDP, 2015).

In addition to the vulnerability of droughts, and the resulting agricultural and fodder shortfalls, many rural women live in a state of extreme poverty.

The Tafawa region, like most of Niger’s regions, faces a precarious situation due to the extent of land degradation and preservation of natural resources in sustainable management is hampered by limited access to water, population pressure, and limited capacity to address the challenges of climate change impact (Ministry of Planning, 2017).

In 2012, Niger’s Total Fertility Rate was very high (6.9 children per woman). Explanatory factors include: (i) low level of education; (ii) the population's pre-birth attitude; (iv) low levels of access to family planning.

Unfortunately, in terms of poverty, the region of Tafawa is one of the poorest regions in Niger, with a poverty rate of 82.32 per cent., compared with 23.1 per cent. in Niamey, the capital city. The region has a population density of 40 people per km².

The region benefits from the following: (i) agricultural potential, mainly in Uprise Gourma and Tafawa; (ii) mineral resources such as gold, iron ore, phosphate, etc.; (iii) large areas of irrigated land; (iv) food security in the region; 31 irrigation schemes (thirty) thirty are for rice production.

The W Regional Park, located on the Niger River valley, comprises a diversity of flora and fauna. Tafawa’s largest cattle herd is in this region (Ministry of Planning, 2017).

The 45 communes in the region have communal councils that deal with various issues, including climate adaptation.

In 2016, the constituency of Tafawa was represented in the Assembly by 24 deputies, out of a total of 137 for the entire country. The constituency is also discussed at the parliamentary level, including the Ministry of Interior (Administrative Subdivisions), Ministry of the Interior.

Programme 8 of the PDS relating to “food security and sustainable agricultural development”, there is a strong focus on women’s empowerment in both sectors (human, technical and financial capacities).

This subprogramme aims to improve rural women's access to land, credit, inputs, extension services, productivity and productive capacity. To this end, it seeks to increase the number of women engaged in domestic chores, increase yields and the productivity and quality of their crops, as well as increasing women’s economic power (Ministry of Planning, 2017).

## RECOMMENDED ACTIONS

The information and data collected via the gender assessments allow to identify a set of equity-related issues and trends that are closely related to the intervention areas of the LOCAD West Africa Programme. These should be taken into account when contributing to address, by mainstreaming equity consideration throughout its design and implementation.

In particular, the following specific measures and actions are recommended to be undertaken to ensure a thorough integration of equity-related considerations as well as a gender-responsive Programme implementation:

1) Institutionalize gender assessments at the local level prior to the disbursement of any project funding and to the design of any project activity.
2) Ensure that funding allocations also enable women to play a role in the design and maintenance of infrastructure projects. In doing so, the Programme intends to act as a good practice to enhance gender equality in public governance and decision-making, including access to finance matters.


---
**Page 166**
---

3) Ensure that gender considerations are embedded across all policy and institutional frameworks that govern the Programme design and its implementation, with adequate capacity and understanding among all stakeholders. In so doing, the Programme intends to serve as a best practice in improving gaps in policies and institutional frameworks to address gender-specific vulnerabilities.

4) Ensure that women are represented in all project activities and are provided with a comfortable space and time to participate in and contribute to all activities. Holding women-only project activities where possible will ensure that women’s voices, needs, and priorities are heard and based on women’s feedback about preferred engagement modalities.

5) By providing women with equal access to information and active and safe engagement opportunities during the design and implementation of project activities, the Programme supports women in becoming more aware of their rights, challenges awareness and adaptation within their communities.

6) Ensure that all Programme communication channels are designed tailored to women’s unique needs and preferences, cultural sensitivities, to ensure that Programme information reaches all the intended target audiences and is adequately understood.

7) Pursue gender-disaggregated data whenever conducting analysis and research, to ensure to capture gender-specific nuances, needs and perspectives in relation to any researched and studied topics.

These recommendations will be used to guide the Programme Gender Action Plan with a view to embed them in as many project activities as possible.

While the Programme explicitly targets men and women, and in its design and implementation it will integrate gender considerations across all policy and institutional frameworks that govern the Programme so as to optimize opportunities for, in particular, social inclusion and gender equality in economic growth, climate resilience, public governance and also ensure that, unintentionally, no-project activity generates unintended negative impacts on men or women.

Social inclusion will be implemented by (i) ensuring at all times a transparent and open implementation approach based on a wide and user-friendly outreach to and engagement of different stakeholders to be consulted in the design and delivery of project activities; (ii) promoting gender equality in decision-making, to foster a more inclusive public governance modality. If and as needed, this will also imply conducting stakeholder-specific consultations, to ensure each stakeholder has a safe venue and convenient means for expressing their views and concerns (such as through focus groups); (iii) collecting stakeholder-specific information and data (including disaggregated by age group).

The continuous monitoring of equity-related matters throughout the Programme implementation is also intended. This will help identify in advance any potential or unforeseen equity-related issues that may surface during its implementation, and that may require adjustments in the design and/or delivery of project activities.

As such, the measures listed above are not intended to be an exhaustive list but rather an initial compilation of recommended measures, to be further strengthened and complemented as needed during the Programme roll-out.

# PROGRAMME CONTRIBUTION TO ENHANCED GENDER SENSITIVITY, EQUALITY AND WOMEN'S EMPOWERMENT

Based on the above, it was decided that all activities identified in the programme are going to be designed and implemented with a gender-sensitive approach and accounting for local cultural sensitivities. Zero tolerance against gender-based discrimination and violence is a core principle of the programme. The programme will have a grievance mechanism, including for offences about sexual exploitation, sexual abuse, and sexual harassment.

The LiCaS Programme expects to be conducive to advance gender sensitivity, equality and women's empowerment in the four countries, both directly and indirectly, in several ways, starting from women's participation in decision-making processes:

Adaptation responses will account for gender-specific vulnerabilities to climate change as identified by the CBA implemented – which account for the distribution of adverse impacts on different groups of people, including women and children.

Awareness and capacity building are sensitive to vulnerable groups' voice and needs, including women's voices, in order to create an inclusive and gender-sensitive civic space that accounts for broader social inclusion.

The Local mechanism increases participatory and gender-sensitive planning processes that leads to the identification of interventions and actions in response to climate change most vulnerable, in a view of distributing adaptation benefits to advance gender equality. LiCaS does that through the following mechanisms to promote gender equality considerations:

### Key few highlights:
- A climate risk assessment methodology that accounts for different stakeholder groups' specifics and respective vulnerabilities.
- Capacity building and awareness raising for gender mainstreaming aspects along the PRCRG deployment cycle (adaptation planning, implementation of investments, M&E and reporting).
- Specific adaptation actions that account for gender mainstreaming aspects along the PRCRG deployment cycle (adaptation planning, implementation of investments, M&E and reporting) that accounts for gender mainstreaming aspects along the PRCRG deployment cycle.
- The ACCO will develop a Gender Equality Impact Assessment Tool (GEIAT) to assess the adaptation benefits of the PRCRG portfolio, providing a M&E tool that can help assess improvements on gender sensitive and responsive adaptation at the local level.
- Institutional strengthening through the development of the Women Economic Empowerment Index (WEEI) to assess the viability of an investment from the point of view of the women's empowerment. The WEEI will be used as a complementary indicator to the REED indicators will be used to support local authorities in assessing the extent to which public adaptation investment projects contribute to women's employment and gender equality.
- These established tools will also benefit the deployment of the Blended Finance Facilities, that will develop a Gender Equality Impact Assessment Tool (GEIAT) to assess the adaptation benefits to gender equality and women empowerment, in addition to BOAD own tools and policy to ensure gender considerations are integrated in all the processes for project origination and financing.


---
**Page 168**
---

> Investments are screened against a climate rationale that should also account for impacts on the most vulnerable populations, including women, and a quota is set on investments (on the framework of GCF) that should explicitly target women (through women's groups, associations, etc.).

> Safeguarding on Sexual Exploitation, Sexual Abuse, and Sexual Harassment (SEAH) are integrated in the ESS checklist (see Annex 6) and will be applied to GCF-financed activities – which include all projects and programmes financed by GCF. The safeguarding measures will be implemented at all stages of the project/programme's operations. These internationally accepted levels of safeguards will be implemented throughout the execution of the programme.

| **Component 1: Community-based Planning Frameworks and Processes** | **Description:** The MCHB provides technical assistance to states in developing and implementing a community-based planning framework for maternal and child health services. | **Target Population:** States and local communities. | **Funding Source:** Federal funds provided through the Maternal and Child Health Bureau (MCHB). | **Performance Measure:** Number of states that have developed or revised their MCHB-funded community-based planning frameworks. | **Data Source:** State reports, annual surveys, and program evaluations. |

| **Component 2: Community Health Centers** | **Description:** The MCHB provides funding to support the development of community health centers that provide comprehensive primary care services for pregnant women and young children. | **Target Population:** Pregnant women, infants, and young children in underserved communities. | **Funding Source:** Federal funds provided through the Maternal and Child Health Bureau (MCHB). | **Performance Measure:** Number of new or expanded community health centers that provide comprehensive primary care services for pregnant women and young children. | **Data Source:** State reports, annual surveys, and program evaluations. |
